# Table of Contents.

->CHAPTER I.<-
**From where Jacob Böhme obtained his knowledge.** <note>The original German word is "Wissenschafft." In the 17th century, this did not mean "science" in the modern sense, but rather a systematic understanding or divine insight.</note>

->CHAPTER II.<-
**Jacob Böhme does not despise the Holy Scripture and the Word of God, but rather points toward them.**

->CHAPTER III.<-
**Jacob Böhme was greatly persecuted.** <note>The original German uses "wordē," where the horizontal bar over the 'e' is a standard printing shorthand for the letter 'n'.</note>

->CHAPTER IV.<-
**Why Jacob Böhme disregarded persecution and danger.**

->CHAPTER V.<-
**One should not take offense at Jacob Böhme’s simplicity.** <note>The term "Einfalt" (simplicity) refers to Böhme’s humble background as an uneducated shoemaker, a fact often used by his detractors to question his spiritual authority.</note>

->CHAPTER VI.<-
**One should not call oneself by Jacob Böhme’s name.** <note>This refers to Böhme’s own wish that his followers should not form a separate sect or call themselves "Boehmists," but remains focused on Christ.</note>

->CHAPTER VII.<-
**Jacob Böhme does not incite rebellion.**

->CHAPTER VIII.<-
**Jacob Böhme has been to Dresden.** <note>This likely refers to Böhme's 1624 journey to the electoral court in Dresden to defend his writings before a committee of theologians.</note>

->CHAPTER IX.<-
**Jacob Böhme’s writings.**

# CHAPTER I. <note>original: "CAPUT I."</note>
## Jacob Böhme
### From where he obtained his
### knowledge. <note>The German "Wissenschafft" in the 17th century refers to a systematic body of knowledge or spiritual science, rather than the modern definition of laboratory science.</note>

Concerning this, he writes thus in his works: This (which I am writing) has remained hidden from the world and has never been so completely and fully revealed in any human heart; I myself am far more amazed by this high revelation <note>original: "Offenbahrung"</note> than the reader perhaps will be.

I do not write this for my own glory, for my glory lies in my hope for the future; I am just as much a poor sinner <note>original: "Sünder"</note> as all other men, and I also belong before this mirror <note>In Böhme’s theology, the "mirror" often represents the process of self-reflection and divine recognition where the soul sees its own nature in God.</note>: but rather, I marvel that God wishes to reveal Himself so entirely in such a simple man, and even drives him to write it down, when there would be many better writers <note>original: "Scribenten," implying professional scholars or university-educated authors.</note> who could write and present it much more loftily than I, who am but a mockery and a fool to the world.

<sig>A3</sig>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>This opening chapter presents Jacob Böhme’s humble defense of his writings, where he claims his spiritual knowledge is a direct revelation from God rather than personal achievement.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, divine revelation, spiritual knowledge, humility, religious authorship, the mirror, 17th-century mysticism</keywords>

# CHAPTER I. <note>original: "CAPUT I."</note>

## Jacob Böhme

### From where he has his knowledge. <note>original: "Wissenschafft." In the 17th century, this term referred to a body of knowledge or a branch of learning, often with a spiritual or philosophical connotation, rather than modern empirical science.</note>

About this he writes in his writings
thus: This (which I write)
has remained hidden from the world /
and has not been made so completely
and utterly manifest in any person's heart /
I myself also wonder much more /
than the reader will perhaps wonder /
at the high revelation.
I do not write such things for my own glory /
as my glory stands in my hope for the
future / I am just as much a poor sinner /
as all people / and also belong before this
mirror: but I wonder / that
God wants to reveal Himself so
completely and utterly in such a simple man /
and even drives him
to write such things down / when there
would certainly be many better <term>writers <note>original: "Scribenten." A term derived from Latin referring to professional authors or scholars, often used here to contrast Böhme’s lack of formal training with the academic elite.</note></term> / who
could write it much more loftily
and carry it further than I /
I who am only the world’s mockery and fool.

<vocab>Jacob Böhme, Revelation, writers, sinner, mockery and fool</vocab>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Chapter I, where Jacob Böhme describes the source of his writings as a divine revelation rather than personal merit. He expresses humility, identifying as a "simple man" and a "sinner" while marveling that God chose him over more learned scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, divine revelation, humility, authorship, spiritual knowledge, mirror of the soul, 17th-century mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter I from the previous page. Böhme is describing his internal struggle between his own will and the divine impulse that drives him to write his revelations. The catchword from the previous page was "But" (Aber).</meta>

But I cannot and will not resist
him / for I have often stood in great labor against
him / [doubting] whether it were not his impulse and
will / that he would take such things from me /
but I find / that with my la-
bor against him I have only contributed stones to this
building. But now I have climbed too high /
and must not look back again /
otherwise I feel dizzy / and have yet a small
little ladder until the goal / there it is all my
heart's delight to climb up completely. For when I
ascend / I do not feel dizzy at all /
but when I look back / and want to turn
around / then I feel dizzy and fear
falling. *Aurora, chapter 14, section 38, and following. Compare <note>original: "Confer."</note>
Aurora, chapter 10, section 53. chapter 11, section 67, and following. chapter 6, section 30, 31.
chapter 18, section 28 and following. chapter 19, section 4, and following. chapter 22, section 50,
52. chapter 23, section 8, and following. chapter 25, section 42, and following.*

If you do not understand these writings / then do
not act like *Lucifer*, do not take the
spirit of <term>pride <note>original: "Hoffart." An archaic term for haughtiness or arrogant pride, specifically the kind that led to Lucifer's fall.</note></term> in hand with mockery and
attribute it to the Devil : but seek the
humble heart of God / that will bring a
small mustard seed from the growth of
Paradise into your soul / and if you
persevere in patience / then a large
tree will grow from it / as you may well think /
and as it also happened to this *Author* /
for he is to be regarded as quite a <term>simple person <note>original: "einfältige Persohn." In Böhme's 17th-century context, "einfältig" (simple/guileless) was often a virtue, indicating a person without the "corrupt" intellectual complexity of university-trained scholars.</note></term> compared
to the highly learned. But *Christ*
says:

<summary>Böhme describes his writing process as a divine compulsion that he can no longer turn back from, comparing his spiritual ascent to climbing a ladder where looking back causes vertigo. He advises readers to approach his work with humility rather than the pride of the "highly learned," using the biblical metaphor of the mustard seed.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, divine inspiration, humility, pride, Lucifer, mustard seed, spiritual growth, Aurora</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction or early chapters of Jacob Böhme’s work, following his description of himself as a "simple person" through whom God speaks. The text transitions from describing the author's humility to quoting scripture and explaining the nature of divine "Wisdom" versus worldly scholarship.</meta>

->❁ (0) ❁<-

A 4

[The Lord] says: My strength is made <term>perfect in the weak <note>original: "in den Schwachen mächtig." A reference to 2 Corinthians 12:9.</note></term>: Yes, Father; it was so pleasing to you that you have hidden it from the clever and the wise, and revealed it to the <term>little ones <note>original: "Unmündigen." Literally the "un-mouthed" or "minors," referring to the simple-hearted or spiritually childlike.</note></term>, so that the wisdom of this world should be foolishness before you. Although the children of this world are cleverer in their generations than the children of Light, their wisdom is but a fragile essence: but this <term>Wisdom <note>original: "Weißheit." Böhme often personifies Wisdom as "Sophia," a divine mirror or companion to God.</note></term> remains eternally.

Therefore seek the noble <term>Pearl <note>original: "Perle." In Böhme's imagery, the "Pearl" represents the spark of divine light or the hidden kingdom of God within the soul.</note></term>; it is much more precious than this world. It will never depart from you, and where the pearl is, there your heart will also be. You need not ask further here for <term>Paradise <note>original: "Paradeiß."</note></term>, or the joy and bliss of heaven. Just seek the pearl; when you find it, you find Paradise and the <term>kingdom of heaven <note>original: "Himmelreich."</note></term>, and you will become so learned that you will not believe anything outside of this.

<margin>The Three Principles, chapter 9, section 45</margin>
You might perhaps be anxious and seek this in <term>Art <note>original: "Kunst." In this context, it refers to human academic study, alchemy, or systematic scholarship rather than creative arts.</note></term>, thinking you will find it there. Oh no! You must not; it is not found within it. The <term>Doctor <note>original: "Doctor." A reference to highly educated theologians or university scholars.</note></term> does not know it except by this way, unless he too has found the pearl; then he is a <term>public teacher <note>original: "Publicus." Likely short for "Professor Publicus," meaning an officially recognized or university-sanctioned scholar.</note></term>, greater than I, just as St. Paul was over the other apostles—but in a way of gentleness, as is fitting for the children of God.

<meta>catchword: Many</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that divine wisdom is revealed to the humble rather than the scholarly, urging the reader to seek the "noble pearl" of internal spiritual truth. He cautions that intellectual "Art" or academic titles cannot grant this insight without a foundation in God's grace.</summary>
<keywords>Wisdom, Foolishness, Light, Pearl, Paradise, Kingdom of Heaven, Doctor, Public Teacher, St. Paul, Children of God</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s spiritual autobiography. Having previously introduced the concept of the "noble Pearl" of divine wisdom, he now describes his frustrating search for it through scholarly books before turning inward to the process of spiritual rebirth.</meta>

->☀ (0) ☀<-

I have searched through the writings of many masters,
hoping to find the <term>pearls <note>original: "Perlen." As established on the previous page, Böhme uses the pearl as a metaphor for the spark of divine light or the kingdom of God hidden within the human heart.</note></term> from the
<term>ground of man <note>original: "Grunde des Menschen." A central concept in German mysticism referring to the deepest, most essential part of the human soul where the creature meets the Creator.</note></term>;
but I have not been able to find that for which my <term>soul <note>original: "Seele."</note></term> longed.
I have also found quite contradictory opinions;
I even found some who forbid me from searching;
I do not know on what ground or understanding they do this,
other than that a blind man begrudges the eyes
of the seeing. With all this, my
soul became quite restless within me,
and was in anguish like a woman in labor,
yet nothing was found, until I followed the
words of <term>Christ <note>original: "Christi."</note></term>, who says:
"You must be <term>born again <note>original: "von neuen gebohren werden." A reference to the dialogue between Jesus and Nicodemus in John 3:3–7.</note></term> if
you wish to see the <term>kingdom of God <note>original: "Reich Gottes."</note></term>."
This at first locked up my heart, for I thought
it could not happen in this world,
but only in my departure from this world.
Then first my soul labored in birth,
which would gladly have tasted the pearl,
and in this way gave itself much more vehemently to birth,
until at last a <term>jewel <note>original: "Kleinod." Literally a "little thing of value," used here to describe the precious spiritual realization or gift he received.</note></term> was granted to it.

Accordingly, I will now write for myself as a
<term>memorial <note>original: "Memorial." A written record intended to preserve a memory or lesson for the future.</note></term>,
and as a <term>light <note>original: "Liechte."</note></term> for the seeker;
for Christ says: No one lights a light and
puts it under a bench or a <term>bushel <note>original: "Scheffel." A reference to Matthew 5:15; a bushel was a large basket used for measuring grain.</note></term>,
but sets it on a table, so that all who are in the room

<summary>The author describes his transition from seeking spiritual truth in the books of "masters" to finding it through a personal experience of spiritual rebirth as commanded by Christ. He frames his writing as a "memorial" intended to serve as a light for others who are also searching.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual search, the Ground of man, soul, rebirth, Kingdom of God, Pearl of Wisdom, inner light, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

are/ see from it. And to that end he gives
the seeker the <term>pearl <note>original: "Perle." In Böhme’s theology, the pearl represents the spark of divine light or the hidden wisdom of God restored to the soul.</note></term> / so that he should 
share it with the poor for their <term>health <note>original: "Gesundheit." In this context, it refers to spiritual well-being, wholeness, or salvation.</note></term> / 
as he has very earnestly commanded. <note>The following citations refer to Böhme’s major works: "3. Princ." is *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* (1619).</note> 3. Princ.

Although it is true that it is given and 
revealed to me / it is nevertheless not of my 
understanding and personal knowledge / but the 
knowledge resides in the <term>Spirit of Christ <note>original: "Geiste Christi."</note></term> / 
according to which the <term>hand <note>The "hand" is a frequent metaphor Böhme uses for himself as the writer/instrument of God.</note></term> describes itself 
twofold / as **us** / for it speaks from two 
persons; for two persons do not say **I** / 
but **We** / and speak of two: like a lord / 
who speaks of his person and of his kingdom. 
In the same way / God’s children and servants 
should not say / the knowledge is mine / 
the understanding is mine / but rather give 
God the glory / and in their revealing of the 
<term>wonders of God <note>original: "Wunder Gottes."</note></term> speak of 
two / namely of the Giver and of the Receiver: 
And no one should understand our writing 
in such a way / as if the hand were boasting 
and honoring itself / according to human authority 
and dignity / even though in Christ we are 
of such dignity: But according to the <term>outward man <note>original: "äussern Menschen." A Pauline concept (2 Cor 4:16) referring to the physical, mortal self as opposed to the "inner" spiritual self.</note></term> 
we desire no honor or fame / so that 
the glory is God’s. <note>"40. Fr. Qv." refers to Böhme's *Forty Questions on the Soul* (1620).</note> 40 Questions. Q. 30, 
Compare Q. 1. & 26.

<summary>Böhme explains that spiritual wisdom (the "pearl") must be shared for the benefit of others and emphasizes that his writings come from the Spirit of Christ rather than his own intellect. He uses the metaphor of "two persons" (Giver and Receiver) to deflect personal credit and ensure all glory remains with God.</summary>
<keywords>Pearl of Wisdom, Spirit of Christ, Outward Man, Humility, Divine Revelation, Jacob Böhme, Three Principles, Threefold Life, Forty Questions</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the author as a vessel for divine wisdom. The catchword "That" from the previous page leads into the author's disclaimer regarding his own education and his description of the internal spiritual struggle that preceded his enlightenment.</meta>

->❃ (0) ❃<-

<page-num>0</page-num>

That I should write of myself, as if I were a
great master of <term>Scripture <note>original: "Schrifft." While it means writing generally, in Böhme's context it refers to the scholarly study of sacred texts.</note></term> or art from
the schools of this world—that is not the case; I
am a simple, plain man, and I do not have
my <term>knowledge <note>original: "Erkántnis." This implies an experiential or mystical insight rather than academic learning.</note></term> and high wisdom from
art or through <term>reason <note>original: "Vernunfft." Human intellect or logic, which Böhme often viewed as limited compared to divine revelation.</note></term>; I have
also never sought great art; but from my
youth onward, I sought nothing else than my soul’s
salvation, and how I might
inherit and possess the <term>Kingdom of God <note>original: "Reich Gottes."</note></term>.

But after I found within myself a powerful
opposition, such as the drive in flesh
and blood, and perceived the mighty struggle be-
tween the <term>Seed of the Woman and the Serpent <note>original: "des Weibes und der Schlangen Saamen." A reference to Genesis 3:15, symbolizing the internal battle between the divine spark and the fallen human nature.</note></term>;
so I then at once
set myself so hard in the struggle against the serpent’s
seed and my own corrupt nature—
though through God’s assistance—that
I intended to overcome and break that same innate evil
will and inclination, and to make the love of God in Chris-
to my own, and to hide myself in God’s heart
before the cruel storm-weather of
the wrath of God and the fury of the <term>Devil <note>original: "Teuffels."</note></term>; so
that <term>God’s Spirit <note>original: "Gottes Geist."</note></term> might rule,
drive, and lead me.

I also resolved to hold myself, in my
innate form, as dead, until
God’s Spirit gained a form within me,
and I laid hold of Him; so that through and in
Him I might lead my life.

<summary>The author identifies as a simple man whose wisdom comes from divine insight rather than worldly education, describing his intense internal struggle to overcome his corrupt nature and surrender to the Spirit of God.</summary>
<keywords>humility, spiritual struggle, Serpent's Seed, Kingdom of God, divine revelation vs. reason, Jacob Böhme, spiritual rebirth</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s autobiographical account of his spiritual awakening. The previous page concluded with his intense struggle against his own "corrupt nature" and the "wrath of God." The catchword from the previous page, "wolte" (would/wished), leads into his resolution to surrender his personal will to the divine.</meta>

<page-num>(o)</page-num>

I also resolved to will nothing, except what I recognized in His light and will; He was to be my will and my doing; which, to be sure, was not possible for me, and yet it remained as an earnest resolution, and in a very earnest struggle and battle against myself.

And what happened there, surely none but God and my soul shall know; for I would rather have risked my life than let go of it.

So I wrestled, with God’s assistance, for a considerable while and time for the <term>knight's little wreath <note>original: "Ritter-Kränklein." A symbolic crown of victory in spiritual warfare, representing the breakthrough into divine wisdom and the overcoming of the self.</note></term>: which I afterwards obtained, with the breaking open of the gates of the deep in the <term>Center of Nature <note>original: "Centro der Natur." In Böhme's philosophy, this refers to the foundational spiritual source where the divine and the natural meet.</note></term>, with very great joy, when a wonderful <term>Light <note>original: "Liecht."</note></term> rose upon my soul, which was foreign to <term>wild nature <note>original: "wilden Natur." The unrefined, fallen state of the human soul before it is illuminated by God.</note></term>.

In this, I first recognized what God and man were, and what God had to do with man: which I never understood before, nor ever sought in such a way, nor [previously] longed for: just as my soul [longed] for this Light: but with no prior knowledge of what should or would happen to me; but rather as a simple child.

Before this, I understood little of the high <term>Articles of Faith <note>original: "Glaubens-Artickel." The formal religious dogmas taught by the church.</note></term>, only as is the way of the laity, and much less did I understand Nature; until the Light began to shine for me in eternal Nature; of which I became so very desirous that I began and

<summary>The author describes a period of intense spiritual combat and self-surrender that culminated in a mystical breakthrough, which he calls the "knight's little wreath." This experience provided him with a direct, child-like understanding of God and the "Center of Nature" that exceeded his previous conventional religious knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual struggle, self-surrender, Ritter-Kränklein, Center of Nature, divine illumination, Articles of Faith, Jacob Böhme, mystical experience</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Jakob Böhme’s description of his spiritual illumination. Having previously described the "Light" that rose upon his soul, he now explains his initial impulse to record these revelations as a private "memorial" rather than a book for public consumption. He emphasizes the raw, unpolished nature of his writing, driven by a sudden, overwhelming spiritual force.</meta>

<page-num>(0)</page-num>

I also wanted to write down my <term>insight <note>original: "Erkäntniß." In Böhme’s writing, this refers to a deep, experiential spiritual knowledge or revelation rather than mere intellectual learning.</note></term> as a <term>record <note>original: "Memorial." A personal memorandum or aid to memory.</note></term> for myself. For the Spirit passed through like a flash of lightning and looked into the foundation of eternity; or as a sudden downpour passes by—whatever it hits, it hits; thus it also happened within me: I began to write like a boy in school, and so I wrote according to my insight and zealous impulse continually, and for myself alone.

I did not intend, in all my life, for it to become known to any person; but rather I wanted to keep it with me all my life as a <term>record</term>, although it was always given to me to write of future things; or as if I were standing before many—as if it were a work that was laid upon me, that I must pursue.

I powerfully felt the will of the newly-kindled <term>Light-Spirit <note>original: "Liecht-Geist." The divine spirit of wisdom and clarity that Böhme believed illuminated his mind.</note></term>. But my soul was before and within him like an ignorant child; she walked thus in the <term>rose garden <note>original: "Rosengarten." A traditional mystical and alchemical symbol for a state of spiritual grace, beauty, and divine intimacy.</note></term> of her mother, and acted as a servant in obedience; and it was given to me to sketch everything on paper in a <term>magical manner <note>original: "magische Art." For Böhme, "magic" is the spiritual power of the will and the process by which the invisible spirit manifests into form.</note></term>.

For I wrote only my own mind, as I understood it in the <term>depth <note>original: "Tieffe." Refers to the "Ungrund" or the profound, foundational mysteries of God's nature.</note></term>; and I made no explanation of it; for I did not intend that it should be read—I wanted to keep it for myself. Otherwise, had I known that it would be read, I would have written more clearly.

<summary>Jakob Böhme describes the spontaneous and private nature of his early writing, explaining that he recorded his sudden spiritual flashes as a personal "memorial" without intending for others to read them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Böhme’s account of his spiritual development. On the previous page, he described his initial, almost involuntary impulse to record his sudden "flash" of insight for his own memory. Here, he characterizes this ongoing process as a divine education, likening his soul to a student in a "heavenly school" and contrasting his inner revelation with the dry, external learning of his critics.</meta>

-> ❁ (0) ❁ <-

Likewise, the labor of my spirit within and through this was like a constant exercise, as my soul deepened itself ever longer and more profoundly into the <term>Mystery <note>original: "Mysterium." For Böhme, this refers to the hidden, divine workings underlying all of reality.</note></term> of eternal nature; just like a student who goes to school and practices diligently.

For the Spirit of Light exercised my soul powerfully; as the impartial reader will see therein, how the Spirit practiced and recounted many things quite often, becoming deeper and clearer from one step to the next; it was the true <term>Jacob’s Ladder <note>A reference to the biblical account in Genesis 28:10–19 of a ladder reaching from earth to heaven, which Böhme uses as a metaphor for the soul's gradual ascent into divine understanding.</note></term>, upon which my soul climbed by the will of God; whom it pleased to exercise me thus, and to lead me into the heavenly school, into the <term>Holy Ternary <note>original: "Ternarium Sanctum." A Latin term Böhme uses to describe the holy manifestation of the Trinity within the "earthly" or created realm—the spiritual environment of heaven.</note></term>. Of this <term>Pasquill <note>A pseudonym Böhme uses for his critic, the nobleman Balthasar Tilke. A "pasquil" was a common term for a satirical or defamatory lampoon.</note></term> knows nothing, and his defamatory writings also testify that he understands nothing of it. He writes only as a <term>historian <note>original: "Historicus." Böhme uses this term dismissively to describe someone who possesses only external, "bookish" knowledge of religious history rather than internal, living experience.</note></term> of the school of this world, which I leave to his own credit; but he boasts unfairly of enlightened eyes, because he uses them for the shame of God's children. Preface to the 1st Apology against B. Tilke § 20, 34. Compare § 93 and following, and 300 and following.

My high <term>knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschafft." Here, the word means "wisdom" or "spiritual science" rather than modern empirical science.</note></term> does not make me blessed: for I do not know myself; it is not mine, but rather God’s Spirit knows Himself in me; He lures me to Himself with it; when He departs, then I know nothing.

<summary>Böhme describes his spiritual growth as a gradual ascent of "Jacob's Ladder" into divine mysteries and defends his experiential wisdom against the purely intellectual criticisms of Balthasar Tilke.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Böhme’s defense of his spiritual authority. Following the previous page’s description of his "heavenly school," he now explicitly rejects the need for academic validation, asserting that his "living knowledge" comes directly from the Divine Spirit rather than human instruction.</meta>

-> ❁ (0) ❁ <-

Since I did not receive my <term>knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaft." In the 17th century, this referred to a deep, systematic understanding or "knowing," which Böhme distinguishes from mere academic learning.</note></term> from men, why should I quarrel with men about it for so long? No one can either give it to me or take it away from me. *1st Apology against Tilken, Paragraphs 587, 588.*

It is well known that I am no Doctor from the schools of this world; if it had not been given to me, I would not understand it. Although I did not seek it so highly—and it was given to me without my own awareness—I sought only the <term>Source-Fountain of Christ <note>original: "Brunn-Quell Christi." A metaphor for the life-giving presence and grace of Christ as the origin of all spiritual insight.</note></term>, and I understood nothing of the **Mystery**, of what that might be; but now it has also been granted to me to see the **Essence of all essences**, in which I would surely be blind without God’s Light. *Apology 2 against Tilken, Aphorisms 11, 12. Compare Aphorisms 32, 35, 42.*

I do not have my knowledge from delusion or opinions, as you do; rather, I have a living knowledge in <term>contemplation <note>original: "Beschauligkeit." A state of meditative, inward vision or direct spiritual observation.</note></term> and <term>perceptibility <note>original: "Empfindlichkeit." For Böhme, this is a "felt" or experiential knowledge—a spiritual sensitivity that registers divine truths as clearly as the physical senses register the world.</note></term>. I need no Doctor from the schools of this world for this; for I have not learned it from them, but from **God’s Spirit**; therefore, I fear nothing from your boasting and mocking. *Ibid. Aphorism 53. Compare Aphorisms 72, 78.*

Kind reader, do not let the pen, or the hand of the pen, lead you astray; the Highest has carved it so, and breathed His breath into it, therefore we know such things well

...and [we] recognize it; we do not know such things out of delusion from another’s hand, or through <term>astral inspirations <note>original: "Astrallische Einfälle." A gate has been opened to us in the <term>Sacred Ternary <note>original: "Ternario S." (Ternario Sancto). Should the <term>driver <note>original: "Treiber." So I say before God / and testify before His judgment / where everything shall appear / and everyone shall give an account of his deeds / that I myself do not know / how this happens to me / except that I have the driving will / and know nothing / of what I should write. For when I write / the Spirit dictates it to me in great and wondrous knowledge <term>Erkäntniß: divine insight or experiential knowledge rather than mere academic learning</term> / so that I often do not know / whether I am in this world according to my spirit / and I rejoice greatly in this / as the steady and certain knowledge is then given to me / and the more I seek / the more I find / and ever deeper / so that I also often regard my sinful person as too lowly and unworthy / to touch such mysteries <note>original: "Geheimniß"</note> / where the Spirit then unfurls my banner <term>Panier: a military standard or rallying flag, here symbolizing divine protection and spiritual identity</term> / and says: "Stand fast; you shall live in this eternally and be crowned with it; why are you terrified?" *Second Epistle, Aphorism 9, 10.*

Although I am a poor, simple man / and born of no art <note>In Böhme's time, "art" (Kunst) referred to academic training or human skill as opposed to natural or divine inspiration.</note> of this world / what I have, however, is God’s gift; I have it not from art or studying / but from the Light of Grace / which I alone have sought / and although my beginning was indeed simple / because of my childish understanding / yet since that time **GOD** has worked something in me in His light and opened my childish eyes.

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s explanation of his writing process. And what is even greater / the **Language of Nature** <note>original: "Natur-Sprache." For Böhme, this is the spiritual and sensory language through which the inner essence of all created things is revealed to the soul.</note> has been opened to me / so that I can understand the very greatest mysteries in my **Mother Tongue** <note>original: "Mutter-Sprache." Böhme emphasizes that he writes in German, the language of the common people, rather than the scholarly Latin expected of theologians at the time.</note> / and although I cannot say / I have grasped and learned it / rather for as long / as the hand of God holds over me / I understand it : but if it hides itself / then I do not even know my own work / and have become a stranger to the work of my hands / so that I may see / how utterly impossible it is / to search out and hold **God's Mystery** without His Spirit.

Therefore I ascribe nothing to myself / it is not my work / I also desire no human honor for it / I am only a poor and simple tool / let God do and make what He will / that I also will / and what He does not will / that I also do not / if He wills that I should know it / then I will to know it / but if He does not will it / then I also do not / I will to be nothing and dead / so that He may live in me and work what He wills. 10th Epistle, sections 27, 30.

I have not scraped it together from histories / and thus formed opinions / as the **Babylonian School** <note>original: "Babylonische Schule." This is Böhme’s critical term for institutionalized, academic theology, which he compares to the Tower of Babel because of its constant bickering over words and doctrines.</note> does / where one squabbles over words and opinions : I have through God's grace received my own eyes / and may work for myself in **Christ's Vineyard**. 11th Epistle, section 38.

<summary>Böhme explains that his insights come through the "Language of Nature" and divine grace rather than academic study. He identifies as a simple tool of God, noting that his understanding vanishes as soon as the Holy Spirit ceases to guide him.</summary>

I pray to my God in Christ that He would unlock His heart, so that his soul <note>Likely referring to the recipient of Böhme's letter</note> might see into the ground <term>Grund: the foundational essence or spiritual depth within a person where God resides</term> of my gifts; for truly, I am a simple man and have neither learned this high mystery <note>original: "Mysterium"</note>, nor sought it in such a way, nor known anything of it. I sought only the heart of love in Christ Jesus; but when I attained it, to the very great joy of my soul, this treasure of divine and natural knowledge <note>original: "Erkantnuß"</note> was opened and given to me. With which I have not, until now, made any boast. [40th Epistle, Aphorism 15. Compare 50th Epistle, Aphorism 1, 54].

You can well and rationally recognize within yourselves that the hand of the Lord has, by His will, led me thus far and brought me to such knowledge, with which I have in turn willingly served many people; because I did not receive my science <term>Wissenschafft: in this context, it refers to a structured, revealed knowledge of spiritual and natural systems, rather than modern empirical science</term> from learning in schools and books, but from the great book of all beings <note>original: "dem grossen Buche aller Wesen," a mystical concept suggesting that the entire created universe is a "book" or "mirror" in which God's nature can be read</note>, which the hand of the Lord has in...

<summary>Jacob Böhme describes his spiritual enlightenment not as a product of academic schooling, but as a gift from God found through seeking the "heart of love" in Christ. He identifies himself as a simple man who serves others by sharing insights gained from the "great book of all beings."</summary>
<keywords>divine knowledge, Babylon, simple man, heart of love, Book of All Beings, Jacob Böhme, spiritual mystery, grace</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's description of how the "treasure of divine and natural knowledge" was opened to Böhme. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding the thought that these mysteries were revealed to him by divine grace rather than through formal schooling.</meta>

<page-num>(0)</page-num>

...opened to me. [50th Epistle, Aphorism 1. Compare <note>original: "Confer."</note> 54th Epistle, 55th Epistle].

That you have received something of my gifts, and that you take pleasure in them, read them, and transcribe them, that is not of my own doing. I am also not the one who gives you the understanding and knowledge <term>Erkantnuß: a deep, experiential spiritual knowledge or "recognition" of truth</term>, much less the desire for it; rather, the Spirit of God within yourselves gives it. For if that could happen through me, and I had the power, then I would wish that all people understood it within themselves, and possessed that which has been granted to me, a poor unworthy man, as a divine gift.

Thus it is also not fitting for me to claim these gifts as my own property, much less to accept honor or gifts from the world because of them—except for what might be given by the wealthy out of their abundance for the maintenance of life and for the further tending to this talent <note>Böhme views his spiritual insight as a "talent" in the biblical sense—a divine trust that must be cultivated and shared</note>; yet for this, I do not demand anything from anyone. [65th Epistle, Aphorism 1 and following <note>original: "seqq."</note>].

## CHAPTER II.

# Jacob Böhme does not despise the Holy Scripture and Word of God, but rather points toward them, as can be seen from the following words.

<meta>catchword: Wis</meta>

<summary>Böhme humbles himself by stating that his spiritual insights are gifts from God's Spirit rather than his own intellect, and he introduces a defense of his respect for Holy Scripture.</summary>
<keywords>divine gifts, humility, Spirit of God, spiritual knowledge, Holy Scripture, stewardship of talent</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's defense of Böhme’s writings, moving from the source of his inspiration to the purpose and biblical foundation of his work. It emphasizes that his insights are intended to comfort "fallen man" and align perfectly with Scripture, requiring no external validation from religious institutions.</meta>

-> <image-desc>The page number is centered at the top, flanked by two circular floral woodcut ornaments.</image-desc> <-

<page-num>(o)</page-num>
<sig>C</sig>

We intend to set down the great wondrous deeds of **God** here sharply, loftily, and in an orderly manner, for the comfort of the sick Adam <note>Böhme uses "Adam" to represent all of humanity in its fallen, "sick" state after the expulsion from Eden</note>, who is currently caught in the press <note>original: "Presse"—a metaphor for the winepress of divine trial or the pressure of earthly suffering that refines the soul</note> and must allow himself to be thoroughly scourged; this shall stand against all the gates of the Devil, and also against all factions and sects. This is done in the ground and light given to us by **God**, and furthermore upon the ground of the Holy Scripture, upon the precious words of promise in the Prophets and Psalms, as well as the Apostolic writings. Although we do not cite all their writings here, we will provide enough proof to anyone who is not satisfied with this summary description. [*The Three Principles of Divine Essence*, chapter 17, § 119 <note>original: "Princ." refers to Böhme's work *De Tribus Principiis*</note>].

If you understand these writings correctly, as they are recognized by the author in the grace of **God**, then you understand everything that Moses and the Prophets have written, and also everything that the mouth of Christ has taught and spoken. You need no masks or spectacles <note>original: "Larven oder Brillen"—Böhme’s way of saying one does not need the artificial "lenses" of academic theology or human tradition to see the truth</note> for this; this knowledge <term>Erkäntnuß: a deep, experiential spiritual recognition or "realization" rather than just book-learning</term> does not need to be confirmed by the Antichristian throne <note>A common Reformation-era critique of the institutional Church or Papacy, which Böhme viewed as an "Antichristian" structure that claimed exclusive right to interpret God's word</note>, which says: "Divine order must be confirmed by its throne, regarding what man should teach and believe, for it cannot err." [*The Three Principles*, chapter 18, Aphorism 59].

<meta>catchword: From</meta>

<summary>Böhme asserts that his writings are deeply rooted in Holy Scripture and intended to comfort suffering humanity, claiming that true spiritual understanding requires no validation from institutional religious authorities.</summary>

# On the Distinction Between the Living and the Dead / Resurrection of the Flesh and the Souls.

Regarding these things, Christ tells us that there will be a great distinction: herewith we direct you to the Scripture, for this shall come to pass according to the Scripture; but because this is also unfathomable and unknowable to human reason <note>original: "Vernunfft"—referring to the limited capacity of the human mind without divine illumination</note>, I would not know how to answer you anything more than what the Scripture says. [40 Questions, Question 30].

I admonish you brotherly, as well as my opponent <note>original: "Gegensatz"—likely referring to the orthodox clergy who criticized him</note>, that you would hold the Holy Scripture against my writings, but seek nothing else in the Scripture than the fatherly heart of love of **Jesus Christ**; thus you will surely find from what spirit I have written. [Apology 2 against Tilke <note>Balthasar Tilke was a prominent critic of Böhme's work</note>, Aphorism 326].

**Satire <note>original: "Pasquill"—a lampoon or satirical attack, often posted in public</note>.** Christ directed us to the Word and the mystery, which sustains the hearts of the faithful in faith. But the shoemaker <note>A derogatory reference to Jacob Böhme’s trade as a master cobbler in Görlitz</note> leads us toward sudden raptures and dreams, which rob the faithful hearts of their faith.

-> Answer. <-

Mr. Primarius! <note>original: "Primarius"—the head pastor or chief official of a local church hierarchy</note> I do not lead people away from the preached and written Word; you do me an injustice in this case. But I say that they should [the...]

<keywords>resurrection, Scripture vs. Reason, Jacob Böhme, Balthasar Tilke, shoemaker, satire, Primarius, religious authority</keywords>

...bring the Temple of **Jesus Christ** to the preached or written Word, as a **repentant, hungry soul**, which hears Christ as the **living Word** teaching within itself, through both the literal and the preached word. As David says: *I will hear what the Lord speaks in me* <note>A reference to Psalm 85:8, emphasizing internal revelation over external hearing</note>; and Christ also spoke: *He who is of God hears God's Word.* And to the Pharisees He said: *Therefore you do not hear, because you are not of God.* Also: *You are not my sheep.*

Thus, it is my opinion that a person must have **Divine Ears** (as a repentant soul) if he wishes to hear Christ's voice—whether it be in preaching, in reading, or in speaking. For this is what Christ has taught us, and I believe Him more than all **art** <note>original: "Kunst"—referring here to human intellectual skill, cleverness, or scholarly theology practiced without spiritual inspiration</note>. For **Historical Faith** <term>Historical Faith (Historischer Glaube): A term Böhme uses for a belief that only accepts the literal facts of the Bible as history, without experiencing the living power of Christ within the soul</term> with its knowledge, its tickling of the ears, and its comforts—yet without power and an earnest will—is dead; it is only a shell, and does not reach the state of **childhood** <note>original: "Kindschafft"—refering to the state of being a "child of God" or the spiritual rebirth</note>. There must be earnestness, etc. *Apology against Richter, p. 354.* <note>Gregor Richter was the primary antagonist of Böhme; he was the pastor of Görlitz who famously had Böhme's books burned and sought his exile.</note>

### CHAPTER III.

# Jacob Böhme was greatly per-
# secuted, about which he
# himself writes in many
# places as follows:

<summary>Böhme argues that true faith requires an internal "hearing" of Christ's voice rather than mere intellectual or "historical" knowledge. This marks a transition into the third chapter, which details the historical persecution Böhme faced from the religious authorities of his time.</summary>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues Böhme’s defense of his writings. The catchword "Rich" at the top likely refers to the continuation of the "Richter" apologies in a collected volume.</meta>

-> <page-num>(0)</page-num> <-

Our great misery and evil—that we so venomously persecute, revile, disgrace, and blaspheme one another—is clearly felt in **Tilke's** <term>Tilke: Balthasar Tilke, a contemporary critic and court official who attacked Böhme’s "Aurora"</term> **Lampoon** <term>Lampoon (Pasquill): A satirical or libelous piece of writing used to publicly insult someone</term>. It is easy to see from what kind of **spirit** <term>Gemüth: In Böhme's vocabulary, this refers to the inner mind, disposition, or the "seat" of the soul's desires</term>, knowledge, and will his writing has flowed. He presumes not only to interpret my words poorly, but also to twist my entire meaning into a foreign opinion and declare the author <note>Böhme refers to himself here in the third person</note> to be of the Devil. 

In a most presumptuous, disgraceful, poisonous, malicious, and envious manner, he assigns the author's will, mind, and spirit to the Devil—without knowing who the author is, what he is like, or what kind of spirit’s child he might be; or whether he seeks **GOD** or this world.

He thus presumes to push his own inborn poison into the author's heart, mind, and will. This is all quite terrible, lamentable, and wretched, for he does not even know himself, nor from what spirit he acts with such zeal, or by what impulse he runs.

He refuses to see that his entire writing is nothing but a poisonous lampoon and an evil opinion, for it declares the author through and through to be of the Devil. O, great **Babylonian Holiness**! <note>A term Böhme used to mock the "confusion" and perceived hypocrisy of the institutional church of his day, likening it to the Tower of Babel</note> *1. APOL. against Tilke § 122. compare § 127 and the following; § 220 and the following.*

You senseless, grim, envious Lampoon! You judge me and condemn me against **Christ's** prohibition, He who said:

> Judge not, that you be not judged; condemn not, that you be not condemned: For with whatever judgment you judge, you will be judged; and with whatever measure you mete, it will be measured to you again. <note>original: "Richtet nicht / auf daß ihr nicht gerichtet werdet / verdammet nicht / auf daß ihr nicht verdammet werdet : Denn mit welcherley Gericht ihr richtet / werdet ihr gerichtet werden / und mit welcherley Maaß ihr messet / wird euch gemeßen werden." A paraphrase of Matthew 7:1–2 and Luke 6:37–38.</note>

Why do you dare to spread such a harmful **lampoon** <term>Pasquill: A satirical or libelous piece of writing intended to publicly discredit someone</term> among the people and judge my written, unprinted writings—which I had written only for myself as a **memorial** <term>Memorial: A private record or notebook intended for personal use and memory</term>, and which came to light without my will—and thus condemn me so frivolously in a completely devilish manner? Was it commanded of you, you **mote-judge**? <term>Splitter-richter: A reference to Matthew 7:3; someone who judges the "mote" (splinter) in another's eye while ignoring the "beam" in their own.</term> It was not a matter of the author of the book to you, but rather that you wanted to display your fine, highly intelligent thoughts, showing how you were a master of Scripture and a man of understanding: but I find you in **Babel** <term>Babel: For Böhme, this signifies the "City of Confusion," representing the corrupted, prideful, and divided state of institutional religion</term> with an un-Christian mind; just keep running on like that, and you will be a faithful laborer for our **LORD GOD** in his **vineyard**. <note>Böhme is being deeply sarcastic here, suggesting the critic is actually working against God's true purposes.</note>

You yourself have put in your **abusive lampoon**: One should condemn no one except for coarse vices and sins: but you alone feel free to do what you forbid others; you alone may judge what is hidden.

Had I known that my book was to be read, perhaps it would have been clearer with ope— <meta>catchword: -nen (likely "offenen," meaning "open" or "clearer words")</meta>

> Judge not, so that you are not judged; condemn not, so that you are not condemned: For with whatever judgment you judge, you will be judged; and with whatever measure you measure, it will be measured to you. <note>original: "Richtet nicht / auf daß ihr nicht gerichtet werdet..." — This is a quotation of Matthew 7:1-2.</note>

...did you not instruct me beforehand and teach me something better? Do you not know—I suppose it is not hidden from you—who I am, namely a **simple man** <term>einfältiger Mann: Böhme frequently identified as an uneducated "layman" or shoemaker to contrast his divine inspiration with the "book-learning" of his academic critics</term>, and unlearned at that? If you are now highly learned and an experienced man, should you then treat me so tyrannically and hand me over to the **Devil**?

Where is your **Christian heart**? Show it to me in your **lampoon** <term>Paßquill: A libelous or satirical writing</term>! Should you condemn a simple man? Oh! I would have given you a friendly answer and a good accounting of my affairs, had you acted as becomes a Christian, with friendly instruction.

But your puffed-up mind would not have it; so now, receive the answer to it; you have deserved no better. Since you declare me to be of the **Devil**, so I declare you a **liar**: For I am a **child of God**, and I zealously strive day and night for my **GOD** with pleas for His mercy for Christ's sake, and I firmly believe that I am a child of God and a member of **Christ’s body**. For this reason, I am also quite certain, through the power of the **Holy Spirit**, that He will preserve for me my **deposit** <note>original: "Beylage" — Likely a reference to 2 Timothy 1:12, referring to the faith or soul entrusted to God's keeping</note>, as that noble **pearl-jewel** <note>original: "Perlen-Kleynod" — A reference to the "Pearl of Great Price" from the Parable in Matthew 13, symbolizing the kingdom of heaven or spiritual truth</note> in Christ, until that day.

You proud **Pharisee** <term>Phariſeer: In Böhme's vocabulary, this refers to hypocritical legalists who prioritize outward religious rules over inward spiritual life</term>, you shall not take from me the noble **knightly wreath** <term>Ritterkränzlein: A "little knightly wreath" or garland, symbolizing the spiritual victory and the crown of life promised to the faithful</term> which my **Savior Christ** has placed upon me. If you...

...but if you desire to pull it away <note>original: "abzuziehen" — likely referring to the "pearl-jewel" of faith or the "deposit" mentioned on the previous page</note> / then see to it what you do / and where you remain / so that the **Wrath of GOD** <term>Zorn Gottes: In Böhme's theosophy, the Wrath of God is the "dark fire" or the first principle of nature when it is not tempered by the light of Love</term> does not swallow you up.

I will, however, have warned you as a **Christian** / to refrain from such blasphemy and shameful condemnation : For the **Kingdom of GOD** consists not in our **much-knowing** <note>original: "viel wissen" — Böhme's common critique of the "Stone-Gaza" or the "Tower of Babel" of academic theology that lacks spiritual experience</note> / but in an earnest / before **GOD** obedient and humble disposition and will ; so that we are **Christians** not with the mouth alone / but in deed.

A **Christian** has no conflict / but only against himself / against his evil nature : **GOD** says : *Vengeance is mine / I will repay.* We live and are in **GOD** / why then do we want to fight over Him? Let each one judge only himself / and convert himself to **God** : the conflict is always about the **husk** / and the **kernel** <term>Hülse/Kern: A central Böhme metaphor where the "husk" is the outward ritual or literal word, and the "kernel" is the living, spiritual reality within</term> / which belongs to the soul / is left lying.

If one has learned something / then let him use it for **God’s** honor / and his brother’s benefit ; He should not despise it : **GOD** has, in His love / without any of our knowledge or merit / through His **Heart** / out of His great **mercy** / **reborn** us ; we who were all once blind / and knew nothing of our **restoration** <term>Widerbringung: Böhme’s term for the return of man/nature to its original divine state; sometimes associated with the doctrine of universal restoration</term> : We were all under **sin** / and He has granted us the **status of children** <note>original: "Kindschaft" — the state of being a child of God</note> out of grace ; Shall we then now judge one another ourselves?

<summary>Böhme warns his critic that true Christianity is defined by humble obedience and inner transformation rather than intellectual knowledge or sectarian conflict. He argues that believers should focus on their own spiritual growth rather than judging others, as salvation is a gift of grace given while man was still "blind."</summary>
<keywords>Wrath of God, much-knowing, humility, internal conflict, husk and kernel, rebirth, restoration, grace, judging others</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jakob Böhme’s defense against the "Lampoon" (Pasqvill) written by Gregorius Richter. ...it is also the case <note>This completes the thought from the previous page: "...where such blasphemy may be developed / it is also the case..."</note> that in those places where one blasphemes the gift of God and attributes it to the Devil, great punishments hang over them. Great punishment also surely hangs over the earthly man where such earnest repentance <note>original: *Busse* — a central concept in Böhme’s theology, referring to a total internal turning away from the self toward God.</note> is practiced; for he must be killed daily and become a fool to the world if he is to walk the divine path. You see well how I am punished by you on such a path, and not by you alone; rather, you also incite the whole city against my neck with reviling, so that they believe you and punish <unclear>me</unclear>; but it is the mark of Christ <note>original: *Christi Mahl-Zeichen* — Böhme identifies his social persecution with the wounds or "stigmata" of Christ, suggesting that suffering for the truth is a sign of divine favor.</note>. *Apology against Richter*. P. 340. 341.

The Lampoon <note>original: *Pasqvill* — Böhme is quoting Richter’s derogatory pamphlet.</note> further says: God will wonderfully rescue His honor, and will not let His honor be taken from Him by such fanatical <note>original: *schwermischen* — a derogatory term used by the orthodox church to describe "enthusiasts" or "fanatics" who claimed direct inspiration from the Holy Spirit.</note> shoemakers, tanners, tailors, women, priests, and doctors.

**Answer.** I also hold it to be so, and firmly believe it, that God will let no devil take from Him His honor—which He has revealed in us poor humans in *Jesus Christ* with the victory over death and hell—much less a *Head Pastor* <note>original: *Primarium* — the title for the chief clergyman of the city, referring here to Richter.</note>, doctor, priest, shoemaker, tailor, tanner, or woman. Rather, those among these who shall practice repentance and be converted to Him, to them He will give His honor. I do believe that He will Himself rescue His children from all evil and make them blessed.

<meta>This page continues Jakob Böhme’s direct response to the "Lampoon" written by Gregorius Richter. Böhme is refuting the specific theological charges that he denies the eternity of God the Father and that he proposes a "Quaternity" (a four-fold Godhead) instead of a Trinity.</meta>

**Lampoon.** <note>original: *Pasqvill* — the derogatory pamphlet published by Böhme’s primary antagonist, Gregorius Richter.</note> The poison of Arius <note>Arius was a 4th-century priest whose "heresy" (Arianism) claimed that Jesus, the Son, was a created being and not eternal like the Father.</note> is not as bad, though he denied the eternity of the Son of God, nor was it as harmful as this **Shoemaker’s poison**, which takes away the eternity of the eternal Father and, in a blasphemous manner, teaches a **Quaternity** <note>original: *Vierheit* — a four-fold nature. Richter accused Böhme of adding a fourth element to the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit), likely misunderstanding Böhme's concept of the "Wisdom" or the "Mirror" of God.</note> or multiplicity. The Eastern lands were terribly punished with Mohammedan darkness <note>original: *Mahometischer Finsterniß* — a common 17th-century European trope suggesting that the rise of Islam in the East was a divine punishment for Christian heresies like Arianism.</note> because of the Arian heresy. What terrible punishments then must we expect to await us, if we do not, with the necessary earnestness, drive this poison away from our fatherland and root it out entirely?

**Answer.** Mr. Head Pastor <note>original: *Herr Primar.* — referring to Gregorius Richter, the Superintendent or head clergyman of Görlitz.</note>, to this I say a resounding No: you cannot prove for all eternity that I take away the eternity of God the Father, much less that I teach a Quaternity of the Godhead. For a long time, you have judged me without foundation and have interpreted my writings with a foreign understanding <note>meaning: "distorted my meaning through your own lens."</note>; you have said from the **pulpit** that I had written that the Son of God was made of **mercury** <note>original: *Qwecksilber* — Richter likely misinterpreted Böhme's use of alchemical terminology, where Mercury often represented a spiritual principle of movement or the Word.</note>. You must prove that, or it shall eternally remain a falsehood; and in such a falsehood, one might well say "fie upon you." *Apology against Richter, pages 341–342.*

All of God’s works must serve Him; God has used you as an instrument for such publicizing. God has given me to recognize this, and therefore I have patiently endured for a long time your unjust blasphemy and disgrace, in which

...that no one can tear me out of Christ’s hand, nor separate me from the love of Christ; neither height nor depth, neither power nor principality—indeed, no creature shall separate me from the love of Jesus Christ. He, my Savior, has granted to me that I am spiritually minded, as St. Paul says: those who are driven by the Spirit of God are the children of God; for we have not received a spirit of servitude, that we must fear once again, but a spirit of adoption <note>original: *kindlichen Geist*, literally "child-like spirit" or "spirit of sonship."</note>, through which we cry: *Abba*, dear Father! The same Spirit bears witness to our spirit that we are God’s children; if we are children, then we are also heirs—namely, God’s heirs and co-heirs with Christ—if we suffer with Him so that we may also be exalted with Him to glory.

Therefore, I say: I have my knowledge <note>original: *Erkantnuß*—for Böhme, this signifies a direct spiritual revelation or "gnosis" rather than intellectual study.</note> not from men or through men, but from the gift of my Savior, Jesus Christ. --- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s defense against the public attacks of Pastor Gregorius Richter. The previous page ended with Böhme arguing that his writings lead to Christ, contrasting his message of repentance with Richter’s accusations.</meta>

->(0)<-

...[this way] indicates/reeks like dung: this is the true color of the Antichrist, that the lampoon <note>original: *Paßqvill*—a "pasquinade" or libelous pamphlet, referring to the written attacks Richter published against Böhme.</note> spits "fie!" and dung against the repentance <note>original: *Busse*</note> and the very earnest prayers in my little book, and calls it a stench. I now give this to the impartial reader to consider: whether this lampoon of the Primary Pastor <note>original: *Primarii*—referring to Gregorius Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz.</note> was not dictated by an Antichristian spirit? Since he wishes to force that spirit upon me, I will let it be known to whom it truly belongs.

**Lampoon.** Furthermore, the lampoon calls me a perjured cobbler <note>original: *meineidigen Schuster*—Richter frequently mocked Böhme’s humble profession as a shoemaker to undermine his theological authority.</note>, and from the pulpit, he has scolded me as a base scoundrel.

**Answer.** This shameful defamation of character <note>original: *Ehrenrührung*</note> is strictly forbidden in the legal statutes, which state that no one shall revile another in his good name and honor. It is also strictly forbidden in all Imperial Constitutions <note>original: *Reichs-constitutionen*—the laws of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>; no one should write a defamatory lampoon or abusive letter under serious penalty. And he is supposed to be the man who should forbid such things to others, yet he does them himself.

Lord Primary Pastor! I ask you: if I had had such a lampoon printed against you, and had so horribly trifled with <note>original: *geleicht-fertiget*—to treat someone with reckless disregard or to libel them.</note> and reviled you, where would you direct and judge me? Certainly to the gallows, and the author with me. But what should I say to you? May God forgive you! I have [sworn/maintained loyalty] to the Roman Emperor, as well as the Elector...

<meta>catchword: [Elector]al Princes (fürsten)</meta>

<summary>Böhme argues that Pastor Richter’s insults and libelous pamphlets violate both Christian charity and the legal statutes of the Holy Roman Empire. He highlights the hypocrisy of a high-ranking clergyman using the pulpit to defame a common citizen’s trade and character.</summary>

—shows itself and stinks like dung: this is the true color of the Antichrist—that the <term>libel <note>original: *Paßqvill*—a lampoon or satirical pamphlet intended to damage someone's reputation.</note></term> spews "fie!" and "dung" against repentance <note>original: *Busse*</note> and the very earnest prayers in my little book, calling them a stench. **Libel.** Furthermore, the libel calls me a perjured shoemaker, and from the **pulpit** he has scolded me as a base scoundrel.

**Answer.** This shameful defamation of character is strictly forbidden in the legal statutes, which dictate that no one shall revile another’s good name and honor. <meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s direct response to the "libel" (the defamatory pamphlet) issued by Gregorius Richter. Böhme is addressing the specific charge of perjury—a legal accusation that could lead to the loss of all civil rights and social standing in 17th-century Görlitz. ❊ (0) ❊

—[Elector-] Prince of Saxony, my most gracious Lord, as well as to the Honorable Council and the city of Görlitz, I have sworn an oath to be faithful and upright. I have held fast to that same oath until this very hour, and yet you call me a perjured, base man and a scoundrel. I brought my honor and integrity into Görlitz, and you wish to strip them from me. For a perjured man is good enough for no one <note>In the 17th century, a "perjured man" (*meineydiger Mann*) was a legal outcast who had lost his "honor" and could no longer testify in court or conduct business.</note>.

Well then, you shall and must prove that I am such a person! I fly with a solemn complaint to the authorities <note>original: *Obrigkeit*</note> for this reason, and I beg them for justice and protection. But because you crush me, these honor-stripping slanders shall meanwhile rest upon you yourself, until the moment you set my perjury before my very eyes.

Lord Primary Pastor <note>original: *Herr Primar.*—Referring to Gregorius Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz who led the persecution of Böhme.</note>. You have spent this Lenten season <note>original: *Fasten-Zeit*</note> studying how you might bespatter Christ’s children in the entire congregation with the serpent-dung of slander, so that they would become slanderous and revile me. And you did not stop there, but even had such defamatory poison printed so that they might read it at home and tickle themselves with it, so that the Devil might be well served. I ask you yourself: is that not a mark of the Antichrist? Was it not your duty, that you should have [spent] this Lenten season on Christ’s bitter suf— <meta>catchword: den/ (the)</meta>

...contemplating his death and the shedding of his blood, and impressing the same upon your parishioners so that they might also take it to heart; instead, during this **Lenten season** <term>Fasten-zeit: the forty days of fasting and penance leading up to Easter</term>, you have composed two **libels** <term>Paßquill: a defamatory pamphlet or anonymous lampoon used to publicly shame someone</term>, and vented your anger with wretched people, so that you have been forced to leave Christ's suffering and death lying upon the **bed of victory** <note>original: *Siegbette*—likely referring to the cross or the tomb from which Christ rose in triumph</note>, while you yourselves lie beside it in awakened venom. Thus you have become unworthy in the eyes of God. Just reflect for a moment; truly, if one takes this to heart correctly, one's hair might well stand on end from horror and terror—that you occupy God’s place and stand in the stead of the Holy Spirit, yet pour out such poison upon the poor, innocent people; and not only here in **Görlitz**, but you also send your defamatory poison to foreign cities and places and poison them, so that they might also help you in your blasphemy. All of this is a certain sign of the **Antichrist**, which the libel wishes to force upon me. I pray, and he curses me; now let whoever understands this judge where the Antichrist truly resides.

**Libel:** O Christ! The Holy Spirit has anointed you with oil more than your companions and has made you a priest; but you, **Shoemaker** <note>Jacob Böhme was a master shoemaker by trade. **Answer.** The Holy Spirit has anointed Christ and His children with the **oil of joy** <note>A reference to Psalm 45:7 and Hebrews 1:9: "God has anointed you with the oil of gladness."</note> from God...

<meta>This page continues the "Answer" section of Jacob Böhme’s defense against the accusations of Gregorius Richter. It concludes the response to the charge of drunkenness and addresses a new charge regarding Böhme's temperament and laughter compared to the solemnity of Christ.</meta>

One might well find the **Primary Pastor** <term>Primarius: Gregorius Richter, the head minister of Görlitz and Böhme's chief antagonist</term> to be drunk more often in a single week than the shoemaker <note>Böhme refers to himself here by his trade to contrast his humble status with his accuser's high office</note> has been in three years. > Therefore, O man, you are without excuse, whoever you are who judge: for in that which you judge another, you condemn yourself; for you who judge do the very same things; for we know that **God’s** judgment is right upon those who do such things. <note>original: *Darum o Mensch / kanstu dich nicht entschuldigen...* — Böhme quotes the German Luther Bible translation of Romans 2:1-2 to point out Richter's hypocrisy</note>

**Libel.** **Christ** refrained from excessive laughter, but the shoemaker laughs at whatever he speaks.

**Answer.** One does not find that **Christ** laughed, but rather rejoiced in spirit; but that the **Primary Pastor** attributes laughing and fantasizing to the shoemaker, and says he laughs at every word, this is likely a great public untruth and cannot be proven; nor can any truthful man say so. But that nature gives one person more cause to laugh and be friendly than another, while making another into a **Saturnine** <term>Saturnine: in the medical and astrological theories of the time, a "Saturnine" person was thought to be influenced by the planet Saturn, resulting in a gloomy, morose, or "sour" temperament</term> sour-looker, is something the **Primary Pastor** cannot change; he would do well to remain silent about it. If one were to describe him and his foolish antics, which he often performs with laughter during a drinking bout <term>Zeche: a social gathering for drinking, often at an inn or tavern, where Richter was allegedly known to behave unprofessionally</term>...

One should certainly not entrust their soul to such antics, or they would be deceived. what kind of good treasure the Lord **Primary Pastor** <note>original: *Primarius* — referring to Gregorius Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz</note> might have in his heart, since he brings forth such strange things? ***Response.*** **Christ** was a teacher sent from **God**, and yet he often had to withdraw from the **Scribes** and **Pharisees** and go into the wilderness to teach the people. And though they saw the **miraculous signs**, they were still mad and stubborn, and believed that teaching belonged to them alone; and they did exactly the same to him as the Lord **Primary Pastor** does to the **cobbler**. They also called him a wine-drinker and a servant of the wealthy tax collectors. <meta>This page continues the defense of Jacob Böhme, contrasting his humble spiritual "account" with the perceived hypocrisy and hostility of the established clergy (the "High Priests"). It picks up from the previous page's discussion of John the Baptist and the early church's need for private meetings.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

...began <note>This completes the thought from the previous page regarding where John the Baptist and the Apostles started their ministry</note>; also the **Apostles** often had to teach in corners and houses out of fear of the **High Priests**, as the **Acts of the Apostles** testifies. But the **cobbler** <note>Jacob Böhme, who was a shoemaker</note> does not present himself as a preacher; rather, he only gives an account of his gift and knowledge, and often speaks with a pious heart about the way to **Christ**: how we must convert, do penance, and be **born again** <note>original: *neugebohren* — a core concept in Böhme's mysticism involving an internal spiritual transformation</note>; for **Christ** also says:

> Where two or three are gathered in my name, there I am in the midst of them.

The Lord **Primary Pastor** <note>original: *Primarius* — Gregorius Richter, Böhme’s chief antagonist in Görlitz</note> should teach us that when we come together, we should speak of **God** and our salvation, and build each other up in true love; instead, he forbids us from doing so. By this one should test which spirit he serves; a tall glass of wine is dearer to him in gatherings than a heartfelt and godly conversation about the **new birth**. **Christ** had no greater enemies on earth than the **High Priests**; and it remains so even today. For all war and strife come from the disputing and reviling of the **High Priests**, as is now plain to see for whoever has their eyes open—but the day of reckoning is near.

**Lampoon.** <term>Pasquill: A satirical or mocking voice, common in 17th-century polemics</term> **Christ** did not desire royal honor, but the **cobbler**, if only he could, would surely like to be king and **God**, etc.

**Response.** **Christ** came to earth poor,

That he may have mercy on us / and make us rich in heaven / and like his dear angels. 

The **cobbler** <note>Jacob Böhme, a shoemaker by trade, whose humble background was often used by his enemies to discredit his complex theological writings</note> is also poor and despised on earth; but he desires from **Christ** the heavenly royal honor / and to become like the angels; he also desires, in **Christ** and according to the inner ground of his faith, to be deified <note>original: *vergöttet* — a mystical concept common in Böhme’s work, referring to "Theosis" or the process of the human soul being united with and transformed by the divine nature</note> with **Christ** and in **Christ**, so that **Christ’s** divinity may dwell in him and remain eternally; no **Primary Pastor** <note>original: *Primarius* — referring to Gregorius Richter, the chief pastor of Görlitz and Böhme's primary persecutor</note> shall take that from him / Hallelujah.

The star has appeared / which leads the Wise Men to the infant **Jesus**; resist, Devil, as you will / it is in vain / here is body and life. My angelic kingdom <note>original: *Englisch Königreich* — here "Englisch" refers to "angelic" (Engel), not the English nation</note> with **Christ** and the holy angels no one can take from me / and even if the whole world were full of such **lampoons** <term>Pasquills: Publicly posted satirical attacks or libels used to shame or mock an opponent</term>, yet I would keep my kingdom in **Christ** / and tread the **lampoons** underfoot / nor spare my outward life for it; I know a better life / which **Christ** my Lord will give me. Therefore, depart, you vile world / I ask nothing of your honor, goods, or money / our citizenship is in heaven with **Christ** / for He is mine and I am His / the enemy shall not part us / Hallelujah.

She has fallen / **Babylon** the great city on earth / and has become a dwelling place for all devils and unclean spirits. Now is the...

The **Chief Pastor** <note>original: *Primar.* — referring to Gregorius Richter, the high-ranking clergyman who persecuted Böhme</note> surely desires a title. For he has set his high-priestly office and his own person in a **lampoon** <term>Pasquill: A satirical or libelous pamphlet used here to describe Richter's written attacks on Böhme</term> with coarse letters, so that one might see the judge who has power over **Christ’s children**; also, if anyone were to approach him too closely and, in their terror, forget to remove their hat, they might well receive a strange title, if he did not receive outright blows.

**Lampoon**. **Christ** was content with the name of a **Master**; this one, however, wishes to be called a **Doctor**, and also a **new Prophet**.

**Answer**. **Thomas** said to **Christ**: *My **Lord** and my **God***; and **Christ** did not contradict this. Now, there is certainly no higher name than **God**: Therefore, perhaps the **Chief Pastor** reads the **Bible** but rarely, since he does not know that **Christ** bore higher titles than **Master**; and before **Pilate**, **Christ** spoke: *I am indeed a **King***. It truly surprises me, since the **Chief Pastor** has studied so much, that he has not yet come upon those places where **Christ’s** titles are written in **Scripture**.

But as for the **Chief Pastor** saying that the **shoemaker** <note>Jacob Böhme, who worked as a master shoemaker in Görlitz while writing his mystical works</note> wishes to be called **Doctor** and a **new Prophet**, that is merely his own fabrication, as one is accustomed to inventing in such **lampoons**, in the manner of **poets**; the people must not believe everything /

He dismisses the claim that he seeks the title of 'Doctor' or 'Prophet' as mere poetic invention by his critics.</summary>

...to believe; <note>The fragment "ben" likely completes the word "glauben" (to believe) from the preceding page.</note> for even a good man can sometimes err, especially when one is drunk and puts on a **mask** <term>Larve: A carnival mask or disguise. The author uses this as a metaphor for the false "labels" or "titles" the clergy used to dehumanize Böhme.</term>. Then, a person is not recognized, even if he were a **High Priest**. As one goes from one house to another in such a state, people say, "here comes a fool," and some might even go so far as to say it is the **Devil** himself.

But it is not all true; people only give him such titles because they assume such appearances. Afterwards, they become human again once they put away the **mask**. Some go about this way only so that they might reach their **lovers** <note>original: *Bulen* — an archaic term for a sweetheart, though often used here to imply a secret or illicit motive.</note>, creating a foolish excuse for it; but in such dealings, one should not believe the titles. When people say a masked man is this or that beast, **Death**, or the **Devil**, he is merely a prisoner of the **Devil**.

Therefore, one should also not believe it when the **Chief Pastor** <note>Gregorius Richter, the primary antagonist of Böhme in Görlitz.</note> gives titles to the **Shoemaker** <note>Jacob Böhme, who was often mocked by his enemies for his humble profession as a master cobbler.</note> and calls him a **heretic**, **fanatic** <term>Schwärmer: Literally "one who swarms." A derogatory term used by the Lutheran establishment for those they considered religious radicals or enthusiasts who lacked proper theological training.</term>, **fool**, **dreamer**, **enthusiast**, a **loose man**, a **perjured shoemaker**, and the like. These are merely painted-on names, like a **mask** that the **Devil** paints on him so that he may stand in conflict with him. It is not good to believe everything; even if a **High Priest** says it, one may be deceived, for great people also err. After all, the **Pharisees** <note>A Jewish sect in the New Testament often depicted as the scholarly opponents of Jesus; here used to compare Richter’s religious authority to those who rejected Christ.</note> erred regarding **Christ’s** titles; why then should the **Chief Pastor** not also err?

<margin>C 2</margin>
...[it is truly a grave mistake to think I] am [such a man]; the taverns and wine cellars are quite safe from me, for you have treated me in such a way that I do not care to go anywhere: If I were now to go drinking in taverns, how many hundreds of your slanders would I not encounter, which you have poured into the common man?
