*Apologia against Richter, pages 355, 363.* <note>Böhme refers to his own written defense against Balthasar/Gregorius Richter, the "Apologia wider Richtern."</note>

Richter speaks of the lion's claws, by which one shall recognize the shoemaker; <note>Richter used the Latin proverb *ex ungue leonem* ("from the claw, the lion") to suggest Böhme's "monstrous" nature could be seen in even a small sample of his work.</note> Now it is certainly true that one will indeed see the lion's claws on him now, as the Antichristian lion has attacked and scratched him with contempt and abuse; but the blood of **Jesus Christ** has hitherto resisted the lion, otherwise the lion would have long ago torn the shoemaker to pieces. But as for his saying that the shoemaker's teaching is filth <note>original: *Dreck* — dirt, dung, or filth; Richter frequently used vulgar language to describe Böhme's mystical writings.</note>, he thereby calls **Christ’s mind** and Spirit filth; and that one should beware of the shoemaker's disciples; to this I say: I know of no disciples, other than Christian people, where one soul refreshes itself with another: I have no new teaching, but only the old one, which is to be found in the Bible and in the Kingdom of Nature; <term>Reich der Natur</term> <note>A central concept in Böhme’s philosophy, referring to the physical world as a mirror of the divine or spiritual realm.</note> but that he calls his slander **God’s Word**, which one should hear from him alone, and that one should also blaspheme as he does, that I leave to a

<meta>catchword: Christ's</meta>

<summary>Jacob Böhme refutes Pastor Richter’s personal attacks, arguing that his own withdrawal from society is due to the Pastor's slanders and asserting that his mystical writings are rooted in ancient Biblical truths rather than being "new" or "filthy" inventions.</summary>

# The third part of the Pasquil follows.
<note>A "Pasquil" or pasquinade is a form of public lampoon or satirical attack. Here, Böhme is quoting and then answering the insults published against him by Pastor Richter.</note>

## Money for the Journey.
<note>original: *Zehr-Pfennig*. This refers to travel money or "viaticum" given to someone departing. In this context, it is used sarcastically as a "parting gift" of insults as Böhme is forced to leave the city.</note>

**Pasqv.** The city of Görlitz is nevertheless driving you, the shoemaker, away, and bids you to travel to where your writings are highly esteemed. Go quickly now, and move far away, you light-minded, blasphemous mouth; and learn, you miserable man, what misfortune is prepared for you.

**Antw.** Mr. Primary Pastor. <note>original: *Herr Primar*. Gregorius Richter held the office of *Primarius* or chief pastor of Görlitz.</note> Keep this "traveling penny" for yourself and your own ascension; I desire nothing from you. Your light-minded blasphemy will surely follow you as your own traveling penny; do not let yourself long for it. Let him who stands take heed, lest he fall. <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 10:12.</note>

Though you persecute me now and intend to banish me, who knows who will also persecute you in a short time? The <term>Signature</term> <note>original: *Signatur*. A central concept in Böhme’s philosophy (his *Signatura Rerum*), referring to the internal spiritual essence of a person or thing that is revealed through its outward appearance or actions.</note> is already present; it is fitting that you should first do to others what might later happen to you as punishment. You must indeed fill up your measure; and if you do not repent, the evil spirits may one day sing such a little funeral dirge for you, and bid you go far away from **God’s** countenance. For what you do to me now, you do to **Christ** in his members; <note>Refers to Matthew 25:40: "Whatever you did for one of the least of these brothers and sisters of mine, you did for me."</note> He will do the same to you, unless you soon turn back and repent.

<summary>This page records a sharp exchange where Pastor Richter mocks Böhme’s impending exile from Görlitz, and Böhme responds by warning the Pastor that his own malice will be his undoing in the eyes of God.</summary>
<keywords>Görlitz, shoemaker, Pasquil, viaticum, Gregorius Richter, Jacob Böhme, Signature, repentance, exile</keywords>

**Pasq.** You are just like Oedipus, whom the earth swallowed up; oh, take care that the same does not happen to you: his shadow greatly harmed those who stood near him, but believe me, your shadow severely harms a great many people. <note>The Pastor compares Böhme to the tragic Greek figure Oedipus, suggesting Böhme is a cursed figure whose presence brings ruin to his neighbors. In some traditions, Oedipus was said to have been "swallowed" by the earth at his death.</note>

**Antw.** The shadow of **Saint Peter** <note>original: *S. Petri*. A reference to Acts 5:15, where Peter's shadow was believed to heal the sick.</note> also greatly harmed the Devil; thus, my shadow also only harms the Devil and the **Antichrist**. You perhaps think that my shadow might harm your earthly life because I profess the **Imitation of Christ** <note>original: *Nachfolge Christi*. This refers to the spiritual practice of living according to the example and teachings of Jesus.</note>; for if people were to accept such teaching, they would demand that you also live like the **Apostles of Christ** and follow their example. That does not suit your taste; therefore, you say my shadow harms some—namely the false **clergy**—most cruelly. This may well be true; it might indeed turn out that way. However, I know many spiritual **priests** <note>original: *Priester*</note> whom it will not harm, but rather benefit greatly—those who take their office with true earnestness. But who can account for Time, which brings all things to light? If the **Lord Primary Pastor** <note>original: *Herr Primar.* Referencing Gregorius Richter, the chief pastor of Görlitz.</note> were so sincere in his warning, he would stop his slandering from the **pulpit** <note>original: *Cantzel*</note> and call me to him privately to show me my **error** <note>original: *Irthum*</note>. If I found that he had some truth against me and he showed me a better way, oh, how dearly I would love him and [thank] him faithfully for it... <meta>catchword: "dans" refers to the word "danksagen" (to thank/give thanks), which begins the following page.</meta>

<summary>Böhme rebuts the Pastor's claim that his presence is a curse, arguing that his teachings only threaten corrupt clergy who refuse to live according to the humble example of the Apostles. He concludes by suggesting that a true Christian leader would offer private correction rather than public slander.</summary>

## The third part of the Pasquil follows.

->Provisions for the Journey<-
<note>original: *Zehr-Pfennig*. Literally a "traveling penny" or money for food on a journey; here used sarcastically as a parting insult or "viaticum" for Böhme's exile.</note>

**Pasqv.** The city of **Görlitz** <note>The city in Upper Lusatia where Böhme lived and where the conflict with the clergy took place.</note> nonetheless drives you, the **shoemaker** <note>original: *Schuster*. Richter uses Böhme's trade as a slur to suggest he is unqualified to write on theology.</note>, away, and bids you go where your writings are highly esteemed. Go quickly now, and travel far away, you frivolous, blasphemous mouth; and learn, you miserable man, what misfortune is prepared for you.

**Antw.** **Lord Primary Pastor** <note>original: *Herr Primar*. Referring to Gregorius Richter.</note>. Keep these **provisions** <note>original: *Zehrpfennig*</note> for yourself for your own **ascension** <note>original: *Himmelfarth*. Böhme implies the Pastor's own death/judgment.</note>; I desire nothing from you. Your frivolous blasphemy will likely follow you as provisions; do not be impatient; let him who stands take heed lest he fall.

Though you persecute me and think to banish me, who knows who will likewise persecute you in a short time? The **Signature** <note>original: *Signatur*. A central concept in Böhme's philosophy, referring to the external manifestation of an internal, spiritual essence.</note> is already present; it is fitting that you do to others beforehand what might afterwards happen to you as punishment. You must surely fill up your measure, and if you do not perform **repentance** <note>original: *Busse*</note>, the evil spirits may well one day also sing you such a **little funeral dirge** <note>original: *Grab-Liedlein*</note>, and bid you go far away from God’s countenance. For what you do to me now, you do to **Christ** in his members; that is what he will also do to you, if you do not soon turn back and repent.

**Pasq.** You are just like **Oedipus** <note>In Greek mythology, Oedipus was a tragic figure whose life brought ruin to his city; Richter uses this classical reference to frame Böhme as a source of spiritual pollution or "plague" to the community.</note>, whom the earth swallowed up; ah, beware that the same does not happen to you: his shadow greatly harmed the people who stood by him, but believe me, your shadow harms many of them very severely.

**Antw.** The shadow of **St. Peter** <note>original: *S. Petri*. A reference to Acts 5:15, where the sick were placed in the street so that Peter's shadow might fall on them and heal them. Böhme is reclaiming the "shadow" metaphor from a curse to a blessing.</note> also greatly harmed the Devil; thus my shadow also harms only the Devil and the Antichrist. You perhaps think that my shadow might harm your earthly life; because I profess the **Imitation of Christ** <note>original: *Nachfolge Christi*. A central theme in Böhme’s theology, emphasizing living according to Christ's humble example rather than just following dry religious dogma.</note>, the people who accept such teaching would demand of you that you also should live like the **Apostles of Christ**, and follow their example, and that does not suit your taste; therefore you say my shadow harms some—namely the false clergy—cruelly much; it may well be so, it might turn out that way.

But I know many spiritual **priests**, whom it will not harm, but rather benefit greatly, those who are truly earnest in their office. But who can control time, which brings all things? If the **Lord Primary Pastor** <note>original: *Herr Primar*. Referring to Gregorius Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz.</note> were so sincere with his warning, he would leave off the slandering from the **pulpit** <note>original: *Canzel*</note>, and summon me to him alone, and show me my error—if I were to find that he had something true against me and he showed me a better way, oh how dearly I would love him, and faithfully [to him]...

<summary>In this passage, Böhme defends his "shadow" (his spiritual influence) against Pastor Richter’s accusations, arguing that his teachings only threaten corrupt clergy who refuse to live according to apostolic simplicity. Böhme also critiques Richter's public attacks from the pulpit, suggesting a private, sincere dialogue would have been more Christian.</summary>

...punishment from **God**, and it is near; know this.

**Pasquil.** <note>A "Pasquil" or pasquinade is a satirical lampoon. Here Böhme records the insults hurled at him by his opponent.</note> Oh, well go now and do not return, so that you finally perish; and take rather a **shoe** than a **pen** in your **hand**. <note>Richter mocks Böhme’s former profession as a cobbler, suggesting he should return to making shoes instead of writing theology.</note> March 21, 1624.

**Answer.** **Christ** speaks: When they curse you, then you shall bless; thus you are **children** of the **Most High God**. Since the **Head Pastor** <note>original: *Herr Primarius*; referring to Gregorius Richter, Böhme's chief critic.</note> now wishes a miserable **death** upon me, his **wish** is from the **Devil**, for it runs entirely against **God’s commandment** and **will**, as well as against all honest **reason** and **integrity**. By this one shall recognize the **tree** by its **fruits**, and anyone may well consider from what **spirit** and **disposition** he persecutes me—that he does such a thing to please **Satan** and is no **servant of Christ**.

But because he curses me, I bless him through the **blood** and **death** of our **Lord Jesus Christ**, in the **name of God** the **Father**, and the **Son**, and the **Holy Spirit**, **Amen**.

And I wish for him that this **blessing** might take hold of him, and bring him forth as a **child** and **heir** of eternal **life**, so that he might become my **brother** in the **life** of our **Lord Jesus Christ**, and remain so eternally. **Amen, Amen, Amen!** And I conclude with all my **heart** and **will**. Given at **Görlitz** on **April** 10, the year 1624.

<summary>In this concluding exchange, Jacob Böhme responds to Pastor Richter’s personal insults and death wishes with a formal Christian blessing. He contrasts Richter's malice with the biblical command to bless those who curse, positioning himself as a true follower of Christ against his accuser's spirit.</summary>

### Appendix to the Pasquil. <note>A "Pasquil" or pasquinade is a satirical lampoon or public insult. In this context, Böhme is quoting or summarizing the accusations made against him by his enemies.</note>

No error is so great and hideous that it does not have those who applaud it.
**Answer.** A good person agrees with what is good, and an evil person with what is evil, such as blasphemy. But for those who love **God**, all things must serve for the best; <note>A reference to Romans 8:28.</note> And even if they were stuck in the midst of all errors, they are nonetheless in **God’s** hand, and protected from the liar and the blasphemer; for such things are a terror to them, and they do not like to hear insolent lies spoken or their neighbor slandered.
**Pasquil.** The error, which is small at first, finally becomes great.
**Answer.** **Christ** speaks: The kingdom of **God** is small at first, like a **mustard seed**; afterwards it grows great, like a tree.
**Pasquil.** In the world, the same **comedies** <note>In the 17th century, "comedy" often referred generally to the "drama" or "play" of human affairs and social roles.</note> are played over and over, only with changed times, places, and people.
**Answer.** **God** has worked from eternity and works in eternity, and yet remains always the same one **God**. Amen.
*Apology against Richter, pages 363, 368.* <note>original: *Apol. wieder Richtern.* Böhme refers here to his own formal written defense against the accusations of Pastor Gregorius Richter.</note>

...is contrary to him and for that reason he would like to murder me; and now, because of his cruel persecution, I must stand under the **Cross of Christ** and bear His marks; <note>Böhme compares his suffering to the stigmata or "marks" (Mahlzeichen) of Christ, viewing his persecution as a spiritual trial.</note> for the **Devil** is now pouring out his last poison. *See 51st Epistle, Section 2. See 49th Epistle, 50th Epistle, Sections 2 and 4, regarding the Pasquil. 51st Epistle, Section 2 and following.* <note>original: "Vid. 49 Epiſt. 50 Epiſt §. 2. et 4. vom Paßquill. 51 Ep. §.2 ſeqq."</note>

Because he rages so much, your **Honorable Council** <note>original: *E. E. Rath*, standing for *Euer Edler Rath*. There I am minded to speak the **truth** from the ground up, and to regard no creature, even should it cost my life. Nevertheless, in a Christian manner without other zeal; I would gladly hear the nobleman’s advice on this. For the hour of **Reformation** has come; may **God** direct it to His honor. *52nd Epistle, Sections 1 and 2.*

I inform you that **Satan** is as enraged with us as if we had destroyed all of **Hell** for him—and yet we have only begun, in a few people, to storm his **robber-castle**. <note>original: *Raub-ſchloß*. A metaphor Böhme uses for the spiritual stronghold of evil or the ego that holds the soul captive.</note>

But because we no longer wish to grant him lodging within ourselves, he has become so furious with us that he intends to wipe us off the face of the earth. He does this so that no more such **little herbs** or **lilies** might grow in his supposed garden—the one he planted with **sin** in **Adam**. <note>Böhme frequently uses botanical imagery; "lilies" represent the blossoming of the reborn soul in contrast to the "thorns" of sin.</note> We wish to leave this to our **Lord God**, for He wishes to use us as His **newly planted little roses**. We must stand still with patience and hope under the **Cross** of our **Lord Jesus Christ**, and listen to what the **Lord** will say, and what He intends to do with His **instrument**. <term>instrument (Werckzeuge): Böhme viewed himself not as a traditional author, but as a passive tool or "work-tool" through which the Divine Spirit communicated.</term>

When I returned home from your company, I found the bed **Satan** had prepared, in which he had wanted to lay me, had **God** not prevented it through several pious hearts to whom our way is better known.

For the **Chief Pharisee** and **Primarius** <note>This refers to Gregorius Richter (1560–1624), the head pastor of Görlitz, who was Böhme's fiercest critic and responsible for his temporary exile.</note> has raged so violently against the **printed little book** <note>likely a reference to the unauthorized printing of Böhme's first work, *Aurora*.</note> as if it had murdered his own son and burned all his possessions. Thus, he has poured out a heap of lies against me, along with frivolous defamation of my character, as can be read in his attached public **pasquil**. <term>pasquil: A lampoon or satirical piece of writing, often posted publicly to shame or libel someone.</term>

## Jacob Böhme’s written Defense to the Honorable Council at Görlitz, against the Primarius’s Slander, Lies, and Persecution regarding the printed booklet *On Repentance*.

Noble, Honorable, Respectable, Highly Learned, most Generous, and well-wise Gentlemen. I appear before my Lords, but now as a **Christian**, and ready to give an account of my gifts and knowledge, which I have received solely and alone from **Divine Grace** as a gift.

Concerning my person, I know nothing else to say than that I am a layman and a simple man, and as a **Christian** I have fallen in love with the love of my **Savior**, and He has fallen in love with me, and betrothed Himself to me according to the inwardness of my soul; of which, if it were required of me, I would give an account.

From such a gift I have my knowledge and science <note>original: "Wissenschafft." In the 17th century, this term referred to a systematic body of knowledge or spiritual insight rather than the modern empirical "science."</note>, and not at all from the **Devil**, as I am unjustly slandered; That

<summary>Böhme begins his formal legal and theological defense to the Görlitz City Council, identifying himself as a simple layman whose insights come from divine grace rather than demonic influence.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Görlitz, defense, Gregorius Richter, Repentance, Divine Grace, layman, mysticism</keywords>

...will involve a serious accounting before the judgment seat of **Christ**: As it is written, *Whoever blasphemes the Holy Spirit has no forgiveness forever*; <note>A reference to Mark 3:29. Böhme suggests that by slandering his divinely inspired insights, his opponents risk eternal judgment.</note> yet I wish for my adversary <note>original: "Wiederspart," referring to his chief critic, the Head Pastor Gregorius Richter.</note> the heartfelt mercy of **God**.

I wrote my first book from such knowledge only for myself as a personal record <note>original: "Memorial," used here to mean a private memorandum or spiritual diary.</note>, intending to keep it solely to myself and show it to no man; however, it was taken from me by **Divine Providence** <note>original: "Göttliche Schickung."</note> and given to the **Head Pastor** <note>original: "Herrn Primario," the highest-ranking clergyman in the city.</note>, as the Honorable Council well knows.

In that same book, a **philosophical** and **theosophical foundation** <term>Philosophical and Theosophical Grund: Böhme uses "foundation" or "ground" (Grund) to describe the underlying spiritual and natural laws of the universe that he believed were revealed to him through divine intuition.</term> is described in such words as I was able to understand for myself in my simplicity at that time, and I did not intend for anyone else to read it. The **Head Pastor** has interpreted this same book with a perspective entirely alien to my own, contrary to my meaning, and has slandered it all this time, which I have borne in patience for the sake of **Christ’s honor**.

But when I defended myself against him before the **Ministry** <note>original: "Ministerio," the local assembly of church officials and ministers.</note> and demonstrated my foundation, I was ordered by the **Head Pastor** to write no more in this manner, which I indeed granted; yet at that time, I did not yet understand the way of **God**, or what He intended to do with me. On the other hand, [he] has

...to record the **process** <note>original: "Proceß." E. Rath" (Euer Edelrath). To this I reply that such is by no means the case, and that this was arranged against me out of ill will by only a few—and perhaps indeed through the **Head Pastor's** own instigation—because he perceives that my innocence shall come to light.

For, firstly, I did not have the **little book** <note>original: "Büchlein." This refers to Böhme's first treatise, "Aurora," which circulated in manuscript for years before being printed without his direct involvement.</note> printed myself; secondly, I did not disseminate it into the **Principality of Legnica** myself; rather, the **patron** <note>original: "Patron." Likely referring to Abraham von Sommerfeld or another nobleman who funded the printing of Böhme's works.</note> who had it printed sent it to his friends and acquaintances. Thirdly, I know that his...

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s formal defense to the Görlitz City Council. He is responding to accusations that his writings have caused a scandal among the high-ranking nobility and clergy in the neighboring Duchy of Legnica.</meta>

❁ (o) ❁

...the claim made regarding such danger—as if the **Duke of Legnica** <note>original: "Herzog zu Lignitz." George Rudolf of Legnica (1595–1653) was a known patron of scholars and religious reformers.</note> along with the entire **clergy** were lodging complaints—is not true at all. For I know this much: that the Duke along with several councilors, as well as many of the priests themselves, read it. It is also loved by many **preachers** <note>original: "Prædicanten." In the 17th century, this specifically referred to Protestant ministers who prioritized the sermon.</note> along with several from the **universities** <note>original: "hohen Schulen."</note>, who are exceptionally learned men. It is likewise loved at the **Electoral Court** in **Dresden** and **Saxony** by distinguished lords, as well as by several **Imperial Princes** <note>original: "Reichs-Fürsten." These were sovereign rulers of territories within the Holy Roman Empire.</note> and lords of the **Imperial Cities**—all of which I could prove with many letters.

And I therefore believe entirely that this **trouble** <note>original: "Bad," literally "bath." A metaphorical expression for being in a difficult or "heated" situation.</note> was prepared for me by the **Devil** and his kingdom, because he sees that his kingdom is revealed through it, and that mankind is being guided toward repentance and a **Christian way of life** <note>original: "Christlichen Wandel."</note>.

But because the **Head Pastor** <term>Primarius <gloss>The senior minister or Superintendent of the local church hierarchy</gloss></term> condemns my little book to the fire, I pray and desire for God’s sake that your **Honorable Council** <note>original: "E. E. Raht" (Euer Edelrath).</note> would command him to record my **errors** <note>original: "Irrthümer."</note> point by point from this book and allow me to provide an answer, or to engage in an oral discussion in the presence of several members of the Council. If it should happen that he proves an **error** to me, I will very gladly accept correction and follow him. But if not—because it is already in print—then...

<summary>Böhme defends his work by citing its popularity among high-ranking nobles and scholars, arguing that the Head Pastor's opposition is a demonic attempt to suppress spiritual truth. He requests a formal, point-by-point hearing before the City Council to answer charges of heresy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s defense against the accusations of the Görlitz Head Pastor, Gregor Richter. Böhme is responding to personal attacks on his character and his spiritual writings.</meta>

he may even write against it, if it pleases your **Honorable Council** <note>original: "E. E. Raht" (Euer Edelrath).</note>; there will surely be learned people who will take my side and answer him, even if I myself do not.

Finally, he has denounced me before the entire **congregation** <term>Gemeine <gloss>The local community of believers in the parish</gloss></term>; he cries out that I despise the **Church** and the **Holy Sacraments**, and he has frequently scolded me as a **heretic** <note>original: "Ketzer."</note>, a **fanatic** <term>enthusiast <gloss>original: "Schwermer." A derogatory term used by established clergy for those who claimed direct spiritual revelation outside of church structures</gloss></term>, and a **scoundrel** <note>original: "Holluncken."</note>. He has attacked my well-established honor and my good, honest name, which I have always maintained. He has also attributed things to me which are not true at all, saying that I constantly gorge myself on **brandy** <note>original: "Brandwein."</note>, as well as other wine and beer, like a pig. This is attributed to me against God, honor, justice, and all truth, out of pure **malice** <term>base passions <gloss>original: "Affecten." Strong, irrational emotions or prejudices</gloss></term>, simply to make me hated by the congregation.

<margin>Does not despise the Church and Sacrament</margin>
For, in the first place, I do not despise any churches, because I go into them myself; much less do I despise the **Holy Sacraments**, which I use myself. Rather, I acknowledge the **temple of Jesus Christ** within us—that we should hear Christ teaching in our hearts, according to the teaching of **St. Stephen** <note>A reference to Acts 7, where Stephen argues that the Most High does not dwell in temples made with hands.</note> and the Apostles. Likewise, I have written more clearly regarding the Holy Sacraments than I have ever heard from him upon the **pulpit** <note>original: "Cantzel."</note>. This could be easily proven.

Furthermore, I am not a teacher or a preacher, and I do not preach or teach; rather, I only give

->❁ (0) ❁<-

...only an account of my gift and knowledge, how I came to it; and on my account, no one needs to fear a **following** <term>sect <gloss>original: "Anhang." Böhme is assuring the council he is not trying to start a breakaway religious faction or cult</gloss></term>. For I do not employ my talent with common people, but with doctors, priests, and noblemen, who are learned.

Therefore, I ask the **Honorable and Highly Wise Council** <note>original: "E. E. und hochweisen Raht." The governing body of Görlitz.</note> to take me into proper protection because of such libelous insults and untruthful accusations; for violence and injustice are done to me by such an accusation. I am no slanderer of the **Church or the Sacrament**, much less a **drunkard** <note>original: "Trunckenbold."</note>, but live quite soberly, with praying and meditating in the **Divine gift** <note>Böhme’s term for his spiritual insights and ability to write.</note>. I also appeal to the entire city, and I know that there will be no man who can accuse me of such a thing. However, one might quite often find that very same drunk man with the **Head Pastor** <note>original: "Herrn Primario." Referring to Gregor Richter, the town’s chief clergyman and Böhme's primary antagonist.</note>; while I almost never enter any man’s house without necessity, much less into beer houses or wine cellars, but live solitary and quiet, as is well known to the Honorable Council. **54. Epistle** <note>This indicates the text is Böhme’s 54th Letter.</note>

You should not take offense at my tribulation and persecution, nor fear anything because of it, for it is the **mark of Christ** <note>original: "Christi Mahlzeichen." Likely a reference to Galatians 6:17, identifying his suffering with that of Jesus.</note>. Only look back into the **Scripture** <note>The Bible.</note>, how it went for God’s children—how they were always [persecuted] precisely by those who were supposed to teach God’s ways...

...pursued and killed. For a noble little pearl <note>original: "Perlein." A recurring metaphor in Böhme’s work representing the "pearl of great price" or the inner spark of divine wisdom.</note> has been entrusted to me; God covers it up in such a way that the unworthy do not see it, but instead are blind to it and take offense at the simplicity <note>original: "Einfalt." This refers both to Böhme’s lack of formal academic training and the humble, direct nature of his spiritual message.</note> of the person. Thus, it remains foolishness to them in their own **reason-wisdom** <term>reason-wisdom <gloss>Vernunfft-Weiſheit. But a time is coming very soon when they shall give a very serious account for this.

But that God has given you to recognize what it is and where it comes from—thank Him for that; it has also happened to you out of grace, and because you have humbled yourselves before Him; and even greater grace may befall you if you remain in humility and earnest prayer. I will gladly share my love with you through prayer and **Christ-member-like** <note>original: "Chriſt-glied-lichen." Meaning to act as a fellow limb or member of the collective Body of Christ.</note> works. For it is a pure joy <note>original: "eitele Freude." In 17th-century German, "eitel" meant "pure," "total," or "nothing but," rather than the modern "vain."</note> in my heart; although I must suffer bodily tribulation because of it, yet I rejoice that I see what God the Lord has done through me, a poor human, until now.

Satan may not hinder God's ways: for although it may seem as if he hinders them with such a **murderous outcry** <note>original: "Mordgeſchrey." This refers to the vitriolic public attacks and calls for Böhme's punishment issued from the pulpit by Pastor Richter.</note>, it is nevertheless revealed more and more so that God's children ask after the **Ground** <term>Ground <gloss>Grund. A central concept in Böhme's theosophy, referring to the absolute foundation of divine being and the origin of all things.</gloss></term>.

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s 1624 defense. The previous page ended mid-word with "ve...", likely completing a phrase such as "transformed" or "made similar" (verähnlicht), as it leads into the soul becoming like the image of Christ.</meta>

...become like his image; for Christ must always be persecuted, mocked, and killed; for he is a sign that is spoken against <note>original: "Zeichen/ deme widersprochen wird." A reference to the prophecy of Simeon in Luke 2:34, framing Böhme's own legal and social troubles as a participation in the life of Christ.</note>, but upon whomever he falls, he crushes him and destroys the inherited malice of the serpent <note>original: "Schlangen." Böhme uses this to refer to the "serpent's seed" or the corrupted nature inherited from the Fall of Man.</note>.

If I was meant to teach others this way—and have taught it—why should it make me anxious to walk upon it myself? It happens to me exactly as I have prescribed to others that it should, and it is quite right that it does, for this is the pilgrim-path of Christ. For true Christians are foreign guests <note>original: "frembde Gäste." A central theme in Böhme’s mysticism is that the believer's soul is an exile in the physical world, seeking a return to its divine origin.</note> here and must travel through the Devil’s kingdom into their true homeland, through nothing but the thistles and thorns of God's curse; there must be wrestling and overcoming until we get through; it will be well rewarded. For those who now judge us will be set before our eyes on the Last Day <note>original: "jüngsten Tage." The day of final judgment.</note>, when we shall reign alongside Christ in the judgment over them. What will they want to say to us then? Will they not fall silent and blaspheme themselves, just as they now do to us?

Oh, that they could reflect upon this here and desist! I wish them **God's** mercy, for they know not what they do. They are in greater misery than those whom they persecute, for they are...

<meta>catchword: [poor] (arme)</meta>

<summary>Böhme frames his persecution by the church authorities as a necessary part of the "pilgrim-path" of a true Christian, arguing that suffering and mockery are signs of following Christ. He expresses pity for his persecutors, warning that the roles of judge and judged will be reversed on the Last Day.</summary>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the thought from the previous page, completing the word "persecute" (German: *verfolgen*). It concludes a passage regarding the suffering of Christians and begins an excerpt from Jacob Böhme’s 58th Epistle, written during his period of intense social and legal persecution in 1624.</meta>

...poor prisoners of the Devil <note>original: "Teuffel."</note>, who torments and poisons them in such a way that poison is their life. Therefore they rejoice that they bear the Devil’s thorns and thistles as fruits in God’s wrath <note>original: "Gottes Zorn." In Böhme's cosmology, this refers to the fiery, stern foundation of nature which, without the light of God's love, becomes a source of agony.</note>: which Christ’s children should well take to heart, who long for their true homeland, so that they not only pray to God for themselves for redemption <note>original: "Erlösung."</note>, but also for these their miserable, poor, imprisoned members, that God might also enlighten and lead them here. 58th Epistle, Section 1, 4.

Do not be offended by my persecution <note>original: "Verfolgung." Böhme was frequently targeted by the local Lutheran clergy, particularly Gregorius Richter, for his mystical writings.</note>, and even if the same should happen to you, then think that there is another life, and that they persecute our own enemy <note>Referring to the sinful or worldly nature of the self.</note>, whom we ourselves also hate; they can take nothing more from us than only the shell <note>original: "Hülse." A common Böhme metaphor for the physical body or the external, temporary world.</note> in which the tree has grown, but the tree stands in heaven and Paradise, in the ground of eternity <note>original: "Ewigkeit."</note>, which no devil can uproot. Just let the Devil’s storm-winds blow over it; their activity and tormenting is our growth <note>original: "Wachsthumb." Böhme argues that the friction of opposition actually strengthens the spiritual "tree."</note>.

I have once again been stirred by the movement of God’s wrath from the adversaries, so that I may grow and become great; for only now has my talent <note>original: "Talent." Here, Böhme refers to his spiritual gift and the "talent" from the biblical parable, representing his duty to share his insights.</note> been made manifest to my homeland; the enemy intends it for evil, but he only publishes my talent through it; it is now being powerfully and greatly desired here.

<vocab>Devil, God's Wrath, Redemption, Paradise, Eternity, Growth, Talent, Persecution</vocab>

<summary>Böhme reflects on his persecution, framing it as a necessary process for spiritual growth and the manifestation of his divine "talent." He uses the metaphor of a tree whose shell may be buffeted by storms while its roots remain safe in the eternity of Paradise.</summary>

growth, <note>original: "Wachsthumb." Continued from the previous page.</note> and many a hungry soul has been refreshed thereby, even though the ignorant crowd <note>original: "unweisende Hauffe." (58th Epistle, sections 1 and 4).

Do not be offended by my persecution, and if the same should happen to you, then consider that there is another life, and that they persecute our own enemy <note>Böhme suggests that his enemies are attacking his earthly, mortal self—the part of him that he himself wishes to overcome through spiritual rebirth.</note>, whom we ourselves also hate. They can take nothing more from us than the husk <note>The physical body or outward social standing.</note> in which the tree grew; but the tree stands in **Heaven** and **Paradise**, in the foundation of **Eternity**; no **devil** can root it out. Just let the **Devil's** storm-winds blow over it; their agitation and torment are our growth.

I have once more been moved by the movement of **God's wrath** through my adversaries, so that I might grow and become great; for only now has my talent <note>Refers to the biblical "Parable of the Talents" (Matthew 25:14-30); Böhme views his spiritual insights and writings as a gift entrusted to him by God.</note> been revealed to my **Fatherland**; the enemy means it for evil, but through this, he publishes my talent; it is now very powerfully desired here,

One also sees their "consistency" very beautifully: that which they now call good, they **deny**—

## CHAPTER IV.
# Why Jacob Böhme
### disregarded persecution and danger
### may be seen from his
### following words.

<sig>S</sig>

<summary>Böhme reflects on the violent attacks against his home in Görlitz, viewing the persecution as a spiritual trial and predicting that the "mustard seed" of truth will eventually overcome his enemies.</summary>
<keywords>Görlitz, Gregorius Richter, religious persecution, mustard seed, Jesuits, 64th Epistle, faith under pressure</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of Jacob Böhme’s resolve in the face of persecution. The catchword "schafft" from the previous page's metadata likely completes the word "Ritterschafft" (spiritual knighthood or warfare) which is cut off at the end of this page.</meta>

<page-num>(6)</page-num>

If we reflect within our own **Reason** <note>original: "Vernunfft"; Böhme distinguishes between human logical reasoning and divine illumination.</note> and look upon that which—in consideration of our high **Knowledge** <note>original: "Erkäntnis"; refers to spiritual insight or gnosis granted by God.</note>—the world at **Babel** <note>A biblical metaphor Böhme uses to describe the confused, corrupt state of the organized church and worldly systems.</note> has introduced in this article of which we wish to treat here, wherein the **Antichrist** <note>Böhme uses this term not just for a specific person, but for the spirit of tyranny and dogma in the church that persecutes the true Spirit.</note> has established himself and shows therein his great power, then our reason might well rightly hold us back because of the great danger of the Antichrist's wrath that might befall us. 

But because it appears to us without our [prior] knowledge, we wish to be more obedient to the voice of **God** than to earthly fear, in the hope of finding true delight. And if it should happen that the Antichrist breaks our earthly body (which nevertheless stands in the providence of God, which we should not resist), we still wish to esteem the future life higher than the fragile one; for that future life, if we reach it, is our true homeland, out of which we departed in **Adam**. And the Spirit invites the ears of all people before this **mirror** <note>original: "Spiegel"; a common mystical term suggesting that his writings are a reflection of divine truth for the reader to contemplate.</note>. *The Principles*, chapter 18, § 66. Compare <note>original: "Confer."</note> chapter 14, § 52, 53.

We will not be pleasing to the Antichrist here, much less to his proud **snout** <note>original: "Rüssel"; a derogatory metaphor for the arrogance and animalistic greed of his persecutors.</note>; but because it appears to us as a wonder, we wish to write it down for ourselves as a **memorial** <note>original: "Memorial"; a written record or testimony intended to preserve a spiritual realization.</note> and see how the beginning and end of every thing is, so that we in our **knightly**— <note>The word breaks off here; the catchword "schafft" completes "Ritterschafft," meaning spiritual knighthood or the struggle of the soul.</note>

<summary>Böhme explains that despite the danger of persecution by the "Antichrist" and the institutional church ("Babel"), he must obey the divine voice over human reason. He views his suffering as a path back to the true homeland lost by Adam.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, Knowledge, Babel, Antichrist, Adam, Mirror, Memorial, Spiritual Warfare</keywords>

Although in this world we must expect only mockery, scorn, and even danger to our temporary life because of this **revelation** <note>original: "Eröffnung"; meaning a spiritual opening or disclosure of hidden truths.</note> concerning the **Antichrist** <note>Böhme frequently uses "Antichrist" to refer to the "Spirit of Babel" or institutionalized religious corruption.</note> and his **Beast** <note>A reference to the apocalyptic Beast from the Book of Revelation.</note>; yet the eternal **knighthood** <note>original: "Ritterschaft"; refers to the spiritual warfare or the "noble" struggle of the soul against darkness.</note> in our Savior Christ comforts us, wherein we are to expect our great reward. Should we remain silent? We must speak, even if we were to lose our earthly life because of it. *The Principles*, chapter 15, § 66. Compare the *Second Epistle*, § 6 and following <note>original: "seqq."</note>.

My writings have not sprung or come from **trifles** <note>original: "Tandt"; meaning vanity, toys, or worthless nonsense.</note> or mere opinion, but through such a path as they themselves indicate and declare: namely, from the **knightly struggle** <note>original: "Ritterlichen Kampf"; Böhme's term for the intense spiritual process of rebirth and overcoming the "wrath" of God.</note>. And what I have gained and overcome through it, I have written down for myself as a **memorial** <note>original: "Memorial und Indenck"</note> and remembrance, and also for the sake of those whom God wishes to visit with it and also crown with this **little wreath** <note>original: "Kränzlein"; a mystical symbol of spiritual victory or the soul's union with Wisdom (Sophia).</note>, as it was given to me to recognize. For this reason, I have written according to the high gifts in the light of God, and [treating] the outer **Reason** <note>original: "Vernunfft"</note> as a fool— <note>The text cuts off here. The catchword "rin" suggests the next word is "Närrin," or "foolish woman/maid."</note>

## CHAPTER V.
# On Jacob Böhme’s
### simplicity, one should not take offense.

Regarding this, his words are well worth noting
when he writes as follows:

> When Master Clever-clogs <note>original: "Meister Klügling"; Böhme’s mocking term for a pedantic critic who relies on intellectual pride rather than spiritual revelation.</note> comes across this
> book (*Aurora* <note>Böhme's first major work, written in 1612, which led to his initial persecution by the church authorities.</note>), he will take the
> opposing side, just as the kingdoms of Heaven and
> Hell surge and exist against one another.
> First, he will say I have climbed much too high
> into the Godhead <note>original: "Gottheit"; referring to Böhme’s mystical insights into the nature of God.</note>, and such things do not befit me.
> Next, he will say that since I boast of the
> Holy Spirit, I must also live accordingly
> and prove such things with miracles <note>original: "Wunderwercken."</note>.
> Thirdly, he will say I do this out of a desire
> for fame. Fourthly, he will say I am
> not learned enough for it. Fifthly,
> the great simplicity <note>original: "Einfalt"; in this context, it means lack of formal academic training and a humble, straightforward spirit.</note> of the author will greatly offend him—
> as is the custom of the world,
> to look only at what is high and to take offense at simplicity.

Against those partisan clever-clogs, I will
set the Patriarchs <note>original: "Alt-Väter"; referring to the biblical figures of the Old Testament.</note> of the ancient
world; they too were only lowly, despised

<meta>This page continues Böhme’s defense of his spiritual authority, which he attributes not to personal merit or formal education, but to a divine "impulse" (Trieb) shared by the biblical prophets and apostles. He transitions from a general defense of "simplicity" to a specific warning taken from the preface of his first work, Aurora.</meta>

✵ (0) ✵

G4

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

[and] could teach in the mysteries of God; rather, it all occurred through the impulse <note>original: "Trieb"; Böhme uses this to describe a divine inner drive or urge that compels a person to act or speak beyond their natural ability.</note> of God.

Likewise, I can say, boast, or write nothing of myself, other than this: that I am a simple man, and moreover a poor sinner, and must pray every day: **Lord**, forgive us our debts; and say with the Apostles: **O Lord,** you have redeemed us through your blood. I have also not ascended into heaven and seen all the works and creatures of God; rather, that same heaven is revealed within my spirit, so that I recognize in the spirit the works and creatures of God. Furthermore, the will for this is not my natural will, but it is the impulse of the Spirit; I have also had to suffer many an assault <note>original: "Sturtz"; literally a fall or plunge, but here referring to a spiritual attack or a stumbling block set by the devil.</note> from the devil because of this. For the spirit of man has not come from the stars and elements alone, but there is also a spark from the light and power of God hidden within it.

### Preface to *Aurora*, p. 43.

But if you, like an Epicurean <note>A term Böhme uses for those who prioritize worldly pleasure and intellectual skepticism over spiritual truth.</note> and the devil’s fattened sow, shall mock these things at the devil’s prompting, and shall say, "The fool has not ascended into heaven and seen or heard it; these are fables"—then by the power of my knowledge, I shall have you summoned and called before the solemn judgment of God.

...woman who, in her lust, is always looking around for handsome men to consort with.

   But to see if this is truly the case, just look back, and you will find the foundation. Who was Abel? A shepherd. Who were Enoch and Noah? Simple folk. Who were Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob?    Now, how did our King Jesus Christ come into this world? Poor, and in great sorrow and misery, and He had nowhere to lay His head.

### Conclusion

I thought I was writing only for myself, and intended to keep it to myself until the end. Now it has become manifest and has come into the hands of many people without my knowledge...

# CHAPTER VIII.

## Jacob Böhme has been to Dresden, as can be seen from the following words of his epistles.

**I** <note>original: *Ich*. This matches the catchword from the previous page.</note> have been invited for a conversation with high-ranking people at the Electoral Court of Saxony <note>The Elector of Saxony was one of the most powerful princes in the Holy Roman Empire; for Böhme, a humble shoemaker, to be invited to his court in Dresden was a significant event.</note>, which I have agreed to carry out at the end of the Leipzig Fair: who knows what might happen there—whether the mouth of the impudent blasphemer might be stopped and the truth planted. *50th Epistle*, Section 7.

Next Friday I travel to Dresden, where I am called to the Elector's councilors to confer with them, as well as with Mr. Henckelman, administrator of the <term>laboratory (Labaratorii): A facility at the Dresden castle dedicated to chemical and alchemical research, reflecting the era's fascination with the transformation of matter and medicine.</term> in the castle. God grant grace for this. *57th Epistle*, Section 3.

Today, May 9th, I travel to Dresden, where I have been invited for a conversation by distinguished people at the Electoral Court who also read and love my writings; God grant grace and strength for this; what may transpire there, I will report to you another time. *60th Epistle*, Aphorism 5.

...would still go well, I would be pleased; for my part I thank **GOD**, who leads me wonderfully according to His good pleasure! On the Wednesday before **Christ’s** Ascension, I arrived in **Dresden** in good health, together with Mr. **Melchior Bernt**, at the home of Mr. **Benedict Henckelmann**, His Electoral Highness’s **Chemist** and **Physician** <term>Chymico und Practico: In the 17th century, these roles often combined alchemy, pharmacy, and medicine; Henckelmann was a key figure in the Elector's laboratory.</term>, where all Christian love and friendship is offered to me, and I am very well cared for; there we have many good **conversations** <note>original: "conversationes"</note>.

And among the Elector's councilors—even the most distinguished of them—there are very Christian and loving gentlemen who hold such **theosophical foundations** <note>original: "Theosophischer Grund"; Böhme's term for the mystical understanding of God and the universe.</note> very dear, and who also love and read my writings; for my printed booklet has come into the hands of nearly many **officials** <note>original: "Officierer"</note> and other learned men, who all recognize it as good and a **gift from GOD**. They work and consider how such good writings, which lead man into the **imitation of Christ** <note>original: "Nachfolge Christi"</note>, might be helped and promoted rather than suppressed, as is sadly happening—God help us—in my homeland out of personal hatred <note>Böhme refers to his hometown of Görlitz, where the local clergy, led by Richter, had banned his work and persecuted him.</note>.

And the councilors and scholars regard the **Chief Pastor's** <note>original: "Primario," referring to Böhme’s rival, Gregor Richter.</note> shameful **lampoon** <note>original: "Passquill," a satirical or libelous pamphlet written by Richter against Böhme.</note> as quite strange; some even believe that the wretched evil spirit **dictated** <note>original: "dictiret"</note> it to him. He is also [shunned?] by the priests...

...judge, saying that he is overstepping his office entirely. For Mr. **Henckelmann** has pointed him out to the councilors and scholars, who wonder at the man's folly—that he dares to pour out his **passions** <note>original: "Affecten"; in this context, uncontrolled emotions or prejudices.</note> in **public** <note>original: "in publico"</note> against such a Christian booklet, at which many a pious heart is horrified. They regard it as a punishment from **GOD** that those who ought to teach others are so blind and resist the Truth itself.

Several of the most distinguished councilors have also sent me word of their goodwill, indicating that at the earliest opportunity, they wish to summon me to a **Christian conversation** <note>original: "conversation"; a formal or semi-formal theological discussion.</note>. I am waiting from **this day** <note>original: "von dato"</note> to see what might happen there; but I hope that all shall go well. For I am certain that they love my writings, just as the **Superintendent** <term>Superintendent: A high-ranking Lutheran administrative official, similar to a bishop, who oversees the clergy in a specific district.</term> here, Mr. **Aegydius Strauch**, reads and loves my printed booklet. I also hope it will be published and reprinted here, as has been suggested to me; then the **struggle would be over** <note>original: "hätte der Krieg ein Loch"; literally "the war would have a hole," a period idiom meaning the conflict would be settled or finished.</note>, and I await daily how **GOD** will ordain it.
62. **Epistle** <note>original: "Epist."</note> ¶. 2. 5.

At Mr. **Henckelmann’s** request, I will remain here in **Dresden** with him for about another month, or however long that may be.

...might well think better of it. <note>original: "62. Epist. § 6. 8." – These citations refer to the numbering used in early published editions of Böhme’s collected letters.</note>

Dresden is now a city of jubilee <note>A city in celebration; at this time, Dresden was a center of Lutheran culture and relatively prosperous compared to the war-torn regions.</note> here / just as Prague was in former times / and things proceed magnificently; from Hungary it is reported here as almost a certainty / as Mr. Henckelmann has heard from the highest officers / especially from von Loß / that peace between the Emperor <note>Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II.</note> and Bethlen Gábor <note>original: "Bethlehem Gabor"; the Prince of Transylvania who led several uprisings against the Habsburgs.</note> has been concluded / but the news reports <note>original: "Zeitungen"; refers to early printed newsletters or reports.</note> run quite differently. Sweden recruited soldiers <note>original: "Knechte"; specifically mercenary infantry.</note> here eight days ago / and the English recruitment also continues / time will reveal more.

Please give my greetings to Mr. Hans Rothe / and indicate to him / that he should please tell Mr. Michael Ender / and Mr. Carl Ender / along with my greetings / if he should have the opportunity to see them / that I will stay here for a while, about four weeks / perhaps less / so that he may report it to Mr. Hans Sigmund von Schweinich.

Should there perhaps be an opportunity / and if perhaps foreign letters have arrived for my wife / I ask that they be sent to me / I will certainly answer them / for messengers <note>original: "Bothen"; professional couriers who carried mail between cities.</note> travel from here to many lands. And I commend you into the love of **JESUS CHRIST**! Dated Dresden. <note>original: "62. Epist. aph. 10. 12."</note>

If things were going well for you, it would be pure joy to me; as for me, I thank God, for my cause has until now been in good standing here; may God help further.

On Holy Pentecost <note>Also known as Whit Sunday; a major Christian feast day celebrating the descent of the Holy Spirit.</note> in the afternoon, the Electoral officers—namely the three Lords of Schwalbach and the House Marshal—the well-born and esteemed colonels of the Elector <note>The Prince-Elector of Saxony, Johann Georg I, one of the most powerful rulers in the Holy Roman Empire.</note>—namely the House Marshal, the Master of the Stables, the Grand Chamberlain, and a Councilor—were guests at my host’s home and came there for my sake, to confer with me; which proceeded in love, favor, and good understanding with them. They heard me quite gladly and found my matters to their liking; they also promised me their goodwill and advancement, and offered and desired to converse further with me, for which I wait daily. They also read my printed booklet <note>Likely "The Way to Christ" (Der Weg zu Christo), the only work of Böhme’s published during his lifetime that caused significant stir.</note> with love, which they have also brought before the Elector.

On the Thursday after Pentecost, the well-born and esteemed Lord Joachim von Loß—Privy Councilor to the Imperial Majesty and the Elector, and an officer of the Empire—sent his carriage to fetch me, along with my host Mr. Henckelmann and a Doctor of Medicine, to his Castle Pölnitz <note>Pillnitz Castle, located on the Elbe River near Dresden.</note> (one mile from Dresden) and conferred with me: to which Lord my matters and gifts seemed highly

### CHAPTER IX.
# Jacob Böhme’s
## Writings.

Regarding his writings, Jacob
Böhme writes in the Twelfth Epistolary Letter <term>Sendschreiben; a formal letter intended to be shared among a circle of followers.</term>,
Aphorism 66, as follows:

->1.<-

The *Aurora* <note>Böhme’s first work, *Aurora: Die Morgenröte im Aufgang* (1612), which he describes as a product of his spiritual "childhood" or early enlightenment.</note> rises out of childhood /
and shows you the creation
of all beings / but almost secretly and not
<meta>catchword: enough.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes Böhme's dramatic eyewitness account of a structure or bridge collapsing into the water in Dresden and begins a new chapter cataloging his spiritual writings, starting with his first book, the Aurora.</summary>
<keywords>Dresden bridge collapse, eyewitness account, Letter 66, Aurora, creation of all beings, spiritual childhood</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Jacob Böhme’s theological works, picking up from the description of his first book, the Aurora, and detailing his foundational texts on the nature of God, the soul, and the three principles of reality.</meta>

enough. It explains much magical understanding / for there are several mysteries within it / which are yet to come to pass.

2. A large book of one hundred sheets <term>Bogen; a printer's unit of measurement, here indicating the length of a manuscript or printed volume.</term> / concerning the *Three Principles of the Divine Essence* / and the Essence of all Essences: this is a key and an alphabet for all those / who desire to understand my writings. It deals with the Creation. Likewise: with the eternal birth of the Godhead: With repentance / the justification of man / and his life in Paradise / and with the Fall. Likewise: with the new birth and the Testaments of Christ and with the whole of human salvation / very useful to read / for it is an eye through which to recognize the wonders in the Mystery of God <note>original: "Mysterio GOttes"</note>.

3. A book of the *Threefold Life*, containing 60 sheets / it is a key from above and below to all mysteries / wherever the heart might wish to soar. It shows the whole foundation of the Three Principles and serves everyone according to their own nature: one can find the ground for nearly every question / that reason <term>Vernunft; Böhme often distinguishes between "earthly reason" and "divine wisdom."</term> can devise within it; and it is the most necessary thing / which would serve you well / you would soon grow weary of the books of contention <note>original: "Zanck-bücher"; referring to the bitter theological and sectarian disputes of the era.</note> / if you took this into your heart and mind.

4. *Forty Questions concerning the Soul*, containing 28 sheets / it deals with all that / which a person ought to know.

5. The fifth book has three parts. The first part concerns the Incarnation <term>Menschwerdung; literally the "becoming-human" or the taking on of flesh.</term> of **Christ**. The second part is quite profound, concerning **Christ’s** suffering and death; how we must enter into **Christ’s** death / die and rise again with and in **Christ**: and why **Christ** had to die: explained entirely from the center <note>original: "centro"</note> through the three principles <term>Principia; Böhme’s metaphysical framework consisting of the "dark world" of fire/wrath, the "light world" of love, and the "outward world" of nature.</term> / a very sublime work. The third part is the Tree of Christian Faith / also very useful to read, explained through the three principles.

6. The sixth book or part of these writings consists of six points of the greatest depth; how the principles give birth to one another and harmonize / so that in eternity there is no conflict: and how each exists within itself: and from where conflict and discord arise: from where evil and good originate / introduced entirely out of the Groundless <term>Ungrunde; a central Böhmean concept referring to the absolute, undifferentiated abyss or "nothingness" that precedes the existence of God's will and all created things.</term> / as from nothing into something / as into the ground of nature. This book is such a mystery / though brought to light in a simple, childlike manner / that no reason <term>Vernunft; human intellectual power when not illuminated by divine spirit.</term> will grasp its foundation without **God’s** light. It is a key to the Alpha and Omega <note>original: "A Ω."</note> (to Everything).

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues Böhme's discussion of the circulation of his manuscripts. The previous page ended with the observation that his works were being distributed and sought after by many, even without his active promotion. This page completes the thought and provides further context on his self-image as a writer and the divine timing of his publication.</meta>

<page-num>(o)</page-num>
<header>❋ (o) ❋</header>

...[sent to those] who desire them. Even several distinguished people have offered to have them published in print, which does not please me at present, because **Babylon** is burning <note>Böhme frequently uses "Babylon" to symbolize the confusion, corruption, and impending judgment he saw in the institutionalized churches and the political turmoil of the early 17th century.</note>; but it will have its time. *12th Epistle, section 1.*

I have approached my writings as a student who goes to school, or like a sudden downpour <term>sudden downpour; translating the German "Platzregen," which implies a spontaneous, heavy, and transient burst of rain. Böhme uses this to describe his flashes of divine inspiration.</term> that passes by; what it hits, it hits: such has also been my comprehension until this day. *18th Epistle, section 12.*

These writings of mine are known and have resounded far and wide in many lands, among the high and the low, and also among some highly learned people; may **GOD** direct them to His honor. *21st Epistle, section 3.*

It has progressed so far with my writings that they are read with pleasure and transcribed by many highly learned **Doctors** <term>Doctors; refers to those holding the highest academic degree in theology or law, the intellectual elite of the time.</term>, and also by many of the nobility, and persons of high and low standing, far and wide—entirely without my own impulse or effort, but through **GOD'S** providence <term>providence; "Schickung," suggesting a divine arrangement or destiny rather than human planning.</term>.

I would have liked to share some of them with you this time, but I did not have them at hand, nor could I obtain them in such haste. It is a fact that several tracts <term>tracts; "Tractaten," referring to specific theological or philosophical treatises written by Böhme.</term> have been written, and I hope that many a hungry soul shall be refreshed by them.

For the later writings <term>writings; "Schrifften."</term> are all much clearer and more easily understood than the first ones which the Lord communicated to me.

<summary>Böhme reflects on the widespread, unsolicited distribution of his manuscripts among all social classes and explains his humble, spontaneous writing process. He notes that his later works are more refined and clear than his initial attempts at describing his divine revelations.</summary>
<keywords>Babylon, providence, tracts, spiritual hunger, divine inspiration, manuscript circulation, humility</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the distribution of Böhme’s writings. Having established that his works are circulating by divine providence, Böhme now addresses a specific individual (referred to as "the Gentleman") who has expressed interest in visiting him to discuss his divine insights.</meta>

<page-num>(o)</page-num>
<sig>I 3</sig>

->(o)<-

But should the gentleman take so much trouble as he mentions, and come to me in person, and accommodate himself to me in the **fear of God** and in **divine wisdom**, that would please me; he can have his opportunity with me as he pleases, for I am even now in constant practice with writing.

I have also laid aside my **trade** <term>trade; "Handwerck." Böhme was a master shoemaker by profession but famously set aside his tools to devote himself fully to his spiritual writing as his "calling."</term> for this sake, to serve **God** and my brothers in this calling, and to receive my reward in heaven, even though I must suffer ingratitude from **Babylon** <note>original: "Babel"; Böhme's term for the corrupt and confused state of the institutional churches.</note> and the **Antichrist**. *34th Epistle, sections 16, 19.*

It would be my heart’s desire, since the gentleman reads several of my writings, that they might be understood according to my own conception and meaning: not for my temporal fame—which is in **Christ** <note>original: "Christo"</note> and not my own—but for the sake of our eternal brotherhood, which we shall share together after this life.

Thus I would also gladly share with my dear brothers my **little pearl** <note>original: "Perlein." This refers to the "Pearl of Great Price" (Matthew 13:45-46), a recurring motif in Böhme’s work representing the inner spiritual light or the discovery of the divine within the soul.</note> given to me by **God**, so that they too, alongside me, might bring forth fruit on **God’s** table in **divine knowledge** and love: to work in this way is dearer to me than all the world's temporal fame, honor, and goods.

And although compared to the Lord I am to be regarded as a child who is without understanding, yet has...

<meta>This page continues the 65th Epistle of Jacob Böhme. The previous page ended with the phrase "Time of Roses," and the catchword "Wel-" connects to "Kindheit" to form "Welt-Kindheit" (Worldly Infancy or the Infancy of the World), referring to Böhme's early spiritual development.</meta>

**Worldly infancy** <note>original: "Wel-Kindheit"; Böhme uses this to describe his early, less mature period of spiritual understanding.</note> / when I was still an **A. B. C. student** <note>original: "A. B. C. To that point I had come / before it was finished: Thus Satan hurried to make an end of it; and at that same time inflicted **cross and tribulation** upon me / also enough hostile people upon my neck / intending to rob me of my **noble pearl.** <note>The "noble pearl" is a recurring metaphor in Böhme’s work for the spark of divine wisdom or the "Pearl of Great Price" found within the soul.</note>

I also understood / that they were in so many people's hands / entirely unknown to me / and that many pious and enlightened hearts found their delight in them / and sought therein not poison / but a true way to the **divine life** and **Christian conduct.**

<summary>Böhme recounts how his early writings (specifically the Aurora) were confiscated and suppressed for years by his adversaries, only for him to discover they had been secretly circulating among spiritual seekers.</summary>

...[things] / which in this book are still in the **magical sense** <term>magischen Verstand; referring to a spiritual or symbolic level of understanding that transcends literal logic.</term> / the other writings are presented clearly and brightly enough / more than **Pasquill** <note>original: "Pasqvill"; Böhme uses this name—referring to a traditional figure used for public lampoons—as a mocking nickname for his critic, Balthasar Tilke.</note> requires and understands; but if someone desires more explanation / it shall be granted to him. *In the same place, Aphorism 59 and following.*

Concerning the ***Aurora*** <note>Latin: "The Daybreak," the title of Böhme's first and most famous work.</note> see the first **Apology** <term>Apologia; a formal written defense of his theology against the attacks of local church authorities.</term> against B. Tilke.

Like a **sudden downpour** <note>original: "Platz-regen"; Böhme uses this weather metaphor to describe the overwhelming, spontaneous nature of his divine revelations.</note> passing by / what it hits, it hits / so it also went with the fiery impulse / although my intention was not at all that anyone should read it / I wrote only the wonders of God / which were shown to me / for myself as a **memorial** <term>Memorial; a personal record or spiritual diary intended for his own use rather than for publication.</term> / and it also went out against my will / and was taken from me by force / and published without my knowledge / for I thought to keep it with me for my entire life / and had no purpose with it / unfortunately to become known / as has happened / to such high-ranking people; but the **Most High** / in whose hands and power everything stands / has another purpose for it / as is now coming to light / that / as I have been informed / it is said to be known in many cities and lands / which indeed wonders me / and yet also does not wonder me / for the Lord judg[es]—

<summary>Böhme reflects on his first book, the "Aurora," explaining that it was a private spiritual record never intended for the public, but was taken and circulated against his will. He views its widespread fame as a divine act rather than his own ambition.</summary>

...[God] carries out His work wondrously and playfully, beyond all **Reason** <term>Vernunfft; in Böhme's vocabulary, this refers to the limited, earthly intellect of man, which cannot grasp divine mysteries without spiritual help.</term> / and should He need a **shepherd** <note>Böhme often calls himself a "shepherd" or a "layman" to contrast his lack of a university degree with the high-ranking church officials who criticized him.</note> for this purpose: because academic skill and external Reason will not grant Him place or room / His purpose must nevertheless stand against all the raging of the **Devil**.

I did not intend for anyone else to read it. The Head Pastor has interpreted that same book with a completely **foreign understanding** <note>meaning Richter applied his own rigid theological biases to Böhme’s mystical metaphors</note>, contrary to my intention, and has slandered it all this time. I have borne this in patience for the sake of Christ’s honor. 54. *Aphoristic Letters* 4. *and following.*

# On the Three Principles of Divine Being.

Read my book on the **Three Principles** <term>Three Principles; "dreyen Principien," Böhme’s foundational metaphysical framework describing the three modes of divine manifestation: the dark fire-world, the light world, and the outer visible world.</term> of Divine Being; what are the stakes? You will see whether I am a man, and not seek a devil within me, as you have most unchristianly treated me; which is truly and rightly highly censurable for you. *Preface to the 1st Apology against B. Tilke.* <note>Balthasar Tilke was a court official and writer who attacked Böhme’s theology.</note> *Section 51.*

Within three-quarters of a year, three books were produced: one on the **Three Principles of Divine Being**, that is, on the **Essence of all Essences** <note>original: "Wesen aller Wesen"</note>, where the **Great Mystery** <term>Great Mystery; "Mysterium Magnum," the abyss of divine potentiality or the hidden source from which the universe is manifested.</term> has revealed itself somewhat. There are very fine things within it, far higher than what is contained in this [first] one, which is the first; and I have [sent it] here for Your Worship <note>original: "E. Gestrt.", an abbreviation for "Eure Gestrengigkeit," a formal title of respect for an official.</note> to look over—about 100 sheets. *10th Letter, section 10; compare* <note>original: "confer."</note> *12th Letter, section 66.*

## On the Threefold Life

Read the book on the **Threefold Life of Man**; you will find the eternal Divine Nature, and then the outer nature of stars and elements, described a little more deeply and thoroughly than in your **lampoon** <term>lampoon; "Pasquill," a satirical or libelous writing. Böhme is responding to a written attack on his character and ideas.</term>. You will well see what **Divine Knowledge** <term>Divine Knowledge; "Göttliche Erkäntniß," referring to a direct, spiritual realization of truth rather than mere book-learning.</term> is, as well as what a person should do and refrain from doing, and what faith and salvation are. *Preface to the 1st Apology against B. Tilke. Section 47.*

I wished, in faithful intention, not to hide from the Lord that now, since the New Year, at the request of several scholars and also persons of high rank, I have written a **treatise** <term>treatise; "Tractat," a formal written work dealing systematically with a subject.</term> on the **Election of Grace**, or **God's Will over Mankind**, and have developed it from such a foundation that one will be able to see every **mystery** <term>mystery; "Heimligkeit," referring here to the hidden spiritual truths or secrets of the divine and natural cosmos.</term>, both of the outer visible **elemental** world and the inner hidden **spiritual** world. ### Two Apologies against Balthasar
### Tilcken.

On account of the poisonous **lampoon** <term>Lampoon; "Paßquilles," a piece of writing that or satirizes or libels someone. In this period, theological debates often involved such harsh personal attacks.</term> of the uncomprehending **zealot** <note>original: "Eyverers," describing someone whose religious fervor is misguided or overly aggressive.</note>, I have given twenty-three sheets in response, but have postponed the answer until now so as not to shame the man; I hope that he might perhaps "become seeing" through the instruction of good people. I have also forbidden it to be delivered yet, to see if it might be possible for him to cease from his malice: otherwise, should the answer come to light, he will carry away little glory—contrary to what he likely hopes for—meanwhile, I give this small amount to be considered.

If it is not enough for him to act thus in brotherly love, let him surely believe that where God’s love is, His wrath is also; may this be shown to him so that he might be ashamed of it and wish he had never begun it. But if he is willing to be at peace, then the answer may rest in the known place; he may safely believe that I see further than he understands.

**Lampoon**: As many lines as there are, just as many **blasphemies** are to be found in the **cobbler's books** <note>A derogatory reference to Jacob Böhme’s profession as a shoemaker, used by his academic and clerical critics to dismiss his theological authority.</note>, which horribly stink of **cobbler’s pitch and blacking**: Fie, fie! This little book teaches the **Way to Christ** most earnestly; and indeed, firstly, how man should and must depart from the **godless way** of this world, and enter into right, **true repentance**, putting on Christ in faith, being **born anew** in Christ’s Spirit, being renewed in mind and soul, and following Christ. Secondly, it teaches of true **resignation** <term>Resignation; original: "Gelassenheit," a core Böhme concept referring to the total surrender of the individual self-will to God's will.</term>, how the repentant man...

You say that in every line (of the little book called **The Way to Christ**) there is a blasphemy, and you forbid the people to read it, under pain of eternal punishment.

Dear Mr. Head Pastor <note>original: "Herr Primarius"; referring to Gregor Richter, the chief clergyman of Görlitz and Böhme's primary antagonist</note>, do open your heart and read the little book correctly; you will find something quite different within it. You have never actually read it; I see this from the titles in your lampoon <note>original: "Pasquil"; a satirical or libelous pamphlet</note>. For you list the titles incorrectly, and you have clearly never seen the booklets on **True Resignation** <note>original: "Gelassenheit"; Böhme's term for the soul’s surrender of its own will to God</note> and **The Imitation of Christ** within it. You list *The Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröte"; Böhme’s first and most controversial work</note> as the first book—about which you know little to nothing—and *The Way to Christ* as the second booklet, when it is not actually a single booklet but is rather the general title. And you list the booklet on **Repentance** as the third book, though it is actually the first book in the printed edition, and you have never even seen the last one.

O Mr. Head Pastor! Satan has blinded you and led you into such anger that, because of that wrath, you were unable to read it. Instead, he hurried you immediately toward mockery and blasphemy, for the book is a stench in his eyes; thus you have followed him.

Dear Mr. Head Pastor! Do cast out these evil impulses <note>original: "Affecten"; strong emotional states or passions</note> from yourself and read it rightly; you will find something very different within. Do you not know that God has [led] several people through it...

...such persecutions, one notices that you wish to completely uproot the **Author** <note>original: "Autorem"</note> because of his divine gift. Had you the power, I would no longer be alive. **Apology** against Richter, **page** 344 **and following** <note>original: "seqq."/sequentes</note>.

With this little book on repentance sent herewith, once you have transcribed it, you may well multiply its yield <note>original: "wuchern"; Böhle uses this in the sense of the biblical Parable of the Talents—to put a gift to work so it grows</note>; for it has a great harvest, and is not very offensive to anyone who is a human being and not a beast. **In the same place, Section 7. The cause of such wrath (in Richter) was the published, printed little book on **Repentance** <term>Busse: the spiritual process of remorse and turning back toward God</term> and true **Yieldingness** <term>Gelassenheit: a central Böhmean concept meaning the total surrender of the self-will to God</term>. He believes that such a life and teaching would also be demanded of him, which does not suit his taste, because he sits in the lust of the flesh and serves the idol **Bacchus** <term>Bacho: the Roman god of wine, suggesting the clergy’s alleged fondness for drinking and worldly pleasure</term> in carnal desire.

I see clearly that this little book is entirely contrary to the Devil, and for that reason he would gladly murder me. Because of his cruel persecution, I must now stand under the **Cross of Christ** and bear His **mark** <note>original: "Malzeichen"; a reference to the spiritual "stigmata" or signs of suffering endured for the sake of the Gospel</note>, for the Devil is now pouring out his final venom.
